## Page 1


Approximation Schemes for Subset TSP and Steiner Tree
on Geometric Intersection Graphs
Sándor Kisfaludi-Bak∗
Dániel Marx†
Abstract
We give approximation schemes for Subset TSP and Steiner Tree on unit disk graphs, and more
generally, on intersection graphs of similarly sized connected fat (not necessarily convex) polygons
in the plane. As a first step towards this goal, we prove spanner-type results: finding an induced
subgraph of bounded size that is (1 + ε)-equivalent to the original instance in the sense that the
optimum value increases only by a factor of at most (1 + ε) when the solution can use only the
edges in this subgraph.
1. For Subset TSP, our algorithms find a (1+ε)-equivalent induced subgraph of size poly(1/ε)·
OPT in polynomial time, and use it to find a (1 + ε)-approximate solution in time 2poly(1/ε) ·
nO(1).
2. For Steiner Tree, our algorithms find a (1+ε)-equivalent induced subgraph of size 2poly(1/ε)·
OPT in time 2poly(1/ε) · nO(1), and use it to find a (1 + ε)-approximate solution in time
22poly(1/ε) · nO(1).
3. An improved algorithm finds a (1 + ε)-approximate solution for Steiner Tree in time
2poly(1/ε) · nO(1).
An easy reduction shows that approximation schemes for unit disks imply approximation schemes
for planar graphs. Thus our results are far-reaching generalizations of analogous results of Klein
[STOC’06] and Borradaile, Klein, and Mathieu [ACM TALG’09] for Subset TSP and Steiner Tree
in planar graphs. We show that our results are best possible in the sense that dropping any of (i)
similarly sized, (ii) connected, or (iii) fat makes both problems APX-hard.
∗Aalto University, Espoo, Finland; e-mail: sandor.kisfaludi-bak@aalto.fi.
Supported by the Research Council of Finland, Grant 363444.
†CISPA Helmholtz Center for Information Security, Germany, e-mail: marx@cispa.de

Introduction
Most of the basic combinatorial optimization problems defined on graphs are APX-hard, which means
that there is a constant c > 1 such that, assuming P ̸= NP, there is no polynomial-time approximation
algorithm for the problem with approximation ratio better than c. In particular, this rules out the
existence of a polynomial-time approximation scheme (PTAS), that is, an algorithm that takes and
instance of the problem and an ε > 0 in the input, and outputs a (1 + ε)-approximate solution in
time nf(1/ε) for some function f. However, these problems can still admit a PTAS when restricted to
some structured class of graphs. For example, there is a long line of research showing that several
APX-hard problems admit approximation schemes when restricted to planar graphs (or perhaps even
to bounded-genus or minor-free graphs) [1, 7, 12–14, 16–18, 22–24, 26, 27, 32, 33, 35, 35, 36, 36, 37, 39, 40,
44, 54, 57, 58, 60, 68–70, 79]. One of the most basic tools in the design of such approximation schemes
is a shifting technique attributed to Baker [9], which is widely applicable for problems that are local
in a certain sense, for example Independent Set or Dominating Set. The shifting technique reduces
a planar instance into several instances where the treewidth of the graph is bounded by a function
of 1/ε. Such instances can usually be handled by standard dynamic-programming techniques on tree
decompositions. Often, the shifting strategy leads to an efficient polynomial-time approximation scheme
(EPTAS), which is a PTAS with running time of the form f(1/ε) · nO(1) for some function f.
Klein [69] introduced a contraction-decomposition version of the shifting strategy. This technique
can be used for problems where the solution needs to provide some form of connectivity, for example,
in TSP (find a shortest closed walk visiting every vertex). Typically, the contraction-decomposition
technique allows us to reduce the problem to instances with treewidth O(1/ε), at the cost of an additive
error that is ε times the total size (weight) of the input graph. For problems where the value of the
optimum is at least the size of the graph, such as in TSP on unweighted graphs, this additive error
results in a multiplicative (1 + ε)-approximation. However, we cannot make this assumption for TSP
on edge-weighted graphs, or for problems where the solution can be much smaller than the graph, for
example, in Subset TSP (find a shortest closed walk visiting every vertex of the given terminal set T) or
Steiner Tree (find a shortest tree containing every vertex of the given terminal set T). These problems
were approached by first computing a “spanner”: removing edges in a way that changes the optimum
value OPT at most by a factor of 1 + ε and ensures that the remaining subgraph has total weight at
most f(1/ε) · OPT. This strategy was successful for weighted TSP, Subset TSP, Steiner Tree, Steiner
Forest, and other problems [12,14–16,22,23,27,68–70].
In the realm of geometric problems (in the plane or in Rd for fixed d), PTASs have been achieved
by leveraging the underlying spatial structure to simplify complex instances. For instance, Euclidean
TSP has a celebrated PTAS by Arora [6] that employs a hierarchical partitioning of the Euclidean
plane into a grid-like structure, allowing the optimal tour to be approximated by a tour that interacts
with grid boundaries only a bounded number of times. More broadly, a range of geometric optimiza-
tion problems—including clustering, facility location, and network design—benefit either from simi-
lar spatial decomposition techniques or from other techniques exploiting the metric properties of the
space [1,5,6,8,15,25,30,31,34,36,38,41,47,62,67].
Besides planar graphs and point sets in R2, another equally natural and well-studied 2-dimensional
setting is given by intersection graphs of various planar objects. In these graphs, each vertex corre-
sponds to a geometric object—such as disks, rectangles, or segments—and an edge represents the in-
tersection between two objects. This spatial representation often imposes additional structure that can
be exploited algorithmically. For instance, many NP-hard problems like Independent Set, Dominating
Set, and Vertex Cover, when restricted to intersection graphs of simple geometric shapes (such as unit
disks or similarly sized fat objects), admit PTASs [63]. The key insight is that the geometry enables a
partitioning of the problem into regions with limited complexity, often through techniques analogous
to the shifting strategy or local search methods. Moreover, for more complex settings where the objects
vary in size, shape, or weight, additional layers of techniques are required [2,3,21,56,61,66,75].
While many geometric problems on intersection graphs benefit from these techniques, there is a
1


## Page 5


notable lack of PTAS results for connectivity problems. The most natural open question in this di-
rection is settling if Steiner Tree and Subset TSP admit a (E)PTAS on geoemtric intersection graphs.
Intuitively, the inherent non-locality of connectivity does not seem to allow any simple use of spatial
decomposition techniques. Indeed, as we show later, an (E)PTAS for Steiner Tree and Subset TSP on
unit disk intersection graphs implies an (E)PTAS for the same problem on planar graphs (Theorem 1.8).
Thus these geometric problems are at least as hard as their planar graph counterparts and hence an
EPTAS for them would likely need to be at least as complex as the highly sophisticated spanner con-
structions for the planar versions [24, 68]. It is far from clear if such an EPTAS is possible: there are
cases where a problem on intersection graphs is strictly harder than on planar graphs. There is such
a gap even for very basic problems: for example, Independent Set and (Connected) Dominating Set
admit an EPTAS on planar graphs, but there is no EPTAS for these problems in unit disk graphs (under
suitable complexity assumptions) [45,48,71–73]. The intuitive similarity of Connected Dominating Set
and Steiner Tree can be interpreted as a warning sign, suggesting that the substantial nonplanarity
present in intersection graphs could potentially make Steiner Tree and Subset TSP strictly harder in,
say, unit disk graphs compared to planar graphs.
Our main contribuitions are developing a set of tools to handle intersection graphs in various
algorithmic and combinatorial contexts, and then using these tools to resolve the open questions by
developping EPTASs for Subset TSP and Steiner Tree on geometric intersection graphs. Specifically,
we consider three results on planar graph EPTASs and show that (unlike in the case of Independent
Set etc.), analogous results for geometric intersection graphs do exist. First, for Subset TSP, Klein [68]
presented a way of obtaining a spanner of size at most poly(1/ε) times the optimum, which, together
with the contraction decomposition technique, gives an EPTAS.
Theorem 1.1 (Planar Subset TSP EPTAS via spanner [68]). Given a planar instance (G, T) of Subset
TSP and an ε > 0,
1. (Subset spanner) We can find a subgraph G′ ⊆G such that SubsetTSP(G′, T) ≤(1 +
ε)SubsetTSP(G, T) and |V (G′)| ≤poly(1/ε) · SubsetTSP(G, T) in polynomial time.
2. (EPTAS) We can find an (1+ε)-approximate solution of the instance (G, T) in time 2poly(1/ε)·nO(1).
Building on the Subset TSP result, Borradaile, Klein, and Mathieu [24] obtained a spanner for
Steiner Tree, but with a size exponential in 1/ε. Consequently, the resulting approximation scheme
has double-exponential dependence on 1/ε.
Theorem 1.2 (Planar Steiner Tree EPTAS via spanner [24]). Given a planar instance (G, T) of
Steiner Tree and an ε > 0,
1. (Steiner-tree spanner) We can find a subgraph G′ ⊆G such that StTree(G′, T) ≤(1 +
ε)StTree(G, T) and |V (G′)| ≤2poly(1/ε) · StTree(G, T) in time 2poly(1/ε) · nO(1).
2. (EPTAS) We can find a (1+ε)-approximate solution of the instance (G, T) in time 22poly(1/ε) ·nO(1).
To obtain an EPTAS with better dependence on 1/ε, the structural insights obtained in the spanner
construction can be used in a more efficient way.
Theorem 1.3 (Faster EPTAS for planar Steiner Tree [24]). Given a planar instance (G, T) of Steiner
Tree and an ε > 0, a (1 + ε)-approximate solution can be found in time 2poly(1/ε) · nO(1).
1.1
Our results
Our main algorithmic results are counterparts of the EPTASs of Theorems 1.1–1.3 to the intersection
graphs of unit disks and other objects. Part of our goal was to understand to what extent such EPTASs
are possible, therefore we state the results in a more general form, for intersection graphs of “similarly
sized fat” (not necessarily convex) polygons. (As we show in Lemma A.1, unit disk intersection graphs
2


## Page 6


can be easily expressed by such objects.) We argue that this is a very reasonable and natural class of
objects for these problems: we present a set of lower bound results that show that going beyond this
class in various ways lead to APX-hard problems.
Formally, an object p in R2 is called β-fat for some β ∈(0, 1] if there are balls B1, B2 of radius r1, r2
such that B1 ⊆p ⊂B2 and r1/r2 ⩾β. A finite collection X of objects in R2 is called δ-similarly-sized
for some δ ∈(0, 1] if minx∈X diam(x)
maxx∈X diam(x) ⩾δ. Throughout this article, we will assume that our objects are
β-fat and the objects collections δ-similarly-sized for some universal positive constants β, δ.
Before presenting our generalizations, let us remark that in planar graphs the edge-weighted ver-
sions of Subset TSP and Steiner Tree can be easily reduced to the unweighted versions by subdividing
each edge an appropriate number of times. In case of geometric intersection graphs, defining edge-
weighted problems usually does not make much sense: large cliques can easily appear in intersection
graphs and an edge-weighted clique can be used to represent arbitrary graphs. Vertex-weighted prob-
lems do make sense for intersection graphs, but such problems can be much more challenging com-
pared to the unweighted versions. Already for planar graphs, much less is known about the vertex-
weighted version [49]; in particular, Theorems 1.1–1.3 work only in case of uniform vertex weights.
Therefore, all our results are for the unweighted versions of the problems. In all the algorithmic results,
we assume that an intersection graph is given in the input with a representation. The input size n is
the total size of this representation, i.e., the total number of vertices of the polygons.
Theorem 1.4 (Geometric Subset TSP EPTAS via spanner). Given ε > 0, an intersection graph G
of δ-similarly sized β-fat polygons with its representation, and a set T ⊆V (G) of terminals,
1. (Subset spanner) We can find an induced subgraph G′ ⊆G such that |V (G′)| ≤Oβ,δ(1/ε10) ·
SubsetTSP(G, T) and SubsetTSP(G′, T) ≤(1 + ε)SubsetTSP(G, T) in polynomial time.
2. (EPTAS) We can find a (1 + ϵ)-approximate solution of the instance (G, T) in time
2Oβ,δ(1/ε5 log(1/ε))poly(n).
Theorem 1.5 (Geometric Steiner Tree EPTAS via spanner). Given ε > 0, an intersection graph G
of δ-similarly sized β-fat polygons with its representation, and a set T ⊆V (G) of terminals,
1. (Steiner-tree spanner) We can find an induced subgraph G′ ⊆G such that |V (G′)| ≤2Oβ,δ(1/ε7.5) ·
StTree(G, T) and StTree(G′, T) ≤(1 + ε)StTree(G, T) in time 2Oβ,δ(1/ε7.5)poly(m).
2. (EPTAS) We can find a (1 + ϵ)-approximate solution of the instance (G, T) in time
22Oβ,δ(1/ε7.5)poly(n).
Theorem 1.6 (Faster EPTAS for geometric Steiner Tree). Given ε > 0, an intersection graph G of
δ-similarly sized β-fat polygons with its representation, and a set T ⊆V (G) of terminals, we can find a
(1 + ϵ)-approximate solution in time 2Oβ,δ(1/ε33)poly(n).
We show that the EPTASs for Subset TSP and Steiner Tree on intersection graphs of connected
similarly sized fat objects is optimal in the sense that dropping any of the three conditions makes
both problems APX-hard problems. Additionally, moving to three dimensions also make the problem
APX-hard, even for the simple case of unit balls.
Theorem 1.7 (APX-hardness results). Steiner Tree and Subset TSP are APX-hard in intersection
graphs of:
(a) similarly sized fat (but potentially disconnected) objects, even for unit-diameter 1/3-fat objects with
two connected components,
(b) similarly sized connected (but potentially non-fat) objects, even for unit-diameter objects,
3


## Page 7


(c) connected fat (but potentially differently sized) objects, even if each object is 0.18-fat, and
(d) similarly sized connected fat objects in R3, even if each object is a unit-diameter ball.
To formally show that approximation schemes for intersection graphs imply approximation
schemes in planar graphs, we present reductions of an appropriate form that transfers the existence of
(E)PTASs. This reduction shows that Theorems 1.4–1.6 can be seen as generalizations of Theorems 1.1–
1.3 (but note that the exponents in the poly(1/ε) terms are somewhat weaker in our results).
Theorem 1.8 (Reduction from planar graphs to unit disk graphs). There is a PTAS reduction from
Planar Subset TSP to Unit Disk Subset TSP and from Planar Steiner Tree to Unit Disk Steiner Tree.
New concepts and techniques of independent interest
As the setting of intersection graphs is
siginficantly more general than planar graphs, we need first to develop a number of basic tools that
were not available for intersection graphs. These techniques could be of independent interest for
other problems involving interection graphs. Note that we state these results in a very general form,
considering similarly sized connected fat polygons, making them potentially useful in a wider range
of applications.
• The faces of a planar graph partition the plane into disjoint regions. Such a decomposition is
crucially used in the proofs of Theorems 1.1–1.3, but it is not clear what the intersection graph
analog of such a clean partition would be. We introduce the notion of wireframes, which are pla-
nar graph representations of intersection graphs. Wireframes will be the main structures guiding
our algorithm by decomposing the problem into regions. We also introduce object frames, a pla-
nar intersection graph whose objects are parts of the original objects.
• Contraction decomposition is an important step in planar PTASs, allowing us to reduce the prob-
lem to bounded-treewidth graphs. We show a clean way of proving such results for intersection
graphs: first, we show how a Lipschitz embedding into planar graphs can be obtained, and then
we show that contraction decomposition of the target planar graph can be lifted to the original
intersection graph.
• One obvious difference compared to planar graphs is that intersection graphs of even very simple
objects can contain large cliques. However, we can preprocess the instance with a sparsification
procedure, which, at the cost of (1 + ϵ)-factor increase of the optimum, reduces the maximum
clique size to polynomial in 1/ε (for Subset TSP) or exponential in 1/ε (Steiner Tree).
• Borradaile, Klein, and Mathieu [24] improved Theorem 1.2(2) to Theorem 1.3 using an elaborate
dynamic programming algorithm designed specifically for their setting. We argue that an alter-
native, more streamlined way of doing this is via a reduction to a vertex-weighted Steiner Tree
problem on bounded-treewidth graphs. This change of viewpoint is the starting point in our
proof of Theorem 1.3.
Having these fairly self-contained components at hand seems to be a prerequisite for working
towards Theorems 1.4–1.6. However, we want to emphasize that obtaining these results is only the
start of the journey. The differences between planar graphs and intersection graphs make it necessary
to deviate from the proof of Theorems 1.1–1.3 at every step, introducing new proof ideas that handle the
phenomena specific to intersection graphs. In Sections 2.1–2.3, the paragraphs marked with “(NEW)”
overview these additional proof ideas.
It turns out that perhaps the most significant initial difference between planar graphs and intersec-
tion graphs is that in planar graphs whenever two paths cross, then they share a vertex. For contrast,
consider the unit disk intersection graph in Figure 1(i). There is a unique shortest a −b path and a
unique shortest c −d path. Even though these two paths cross twice, it is not possible to reroute any
of the two paths to the other path without increasing its length (contrary to what we would expect in

Figure 1: (i) Two paths in a unit disk graphs that cannot be shortened: on the blue path from a to b, the
distance of the disk from a is indicated with blue numbers, and on the red c →d path the red number
is the distance from c. (ii) In a planar graph, if C is a cycle where the distance of vertices on the cycle
is less or equal to their graph distance, then we can shorten any path P that intersects C at least twice.
a planar graph). This difference invalidates the main principle behind the structural decompositions
in the proof of Theorems 1.1–1.3. This principle can be briefly formulated as follows (see Figure 1(ii)).
Let C be a cycle such that for any two vertices x, y ∈C, the shorter of the two paths between x and y
on C is a shortest x−y path. Then if P is a path with endpoints outside C and P enters C, then P can
be rerouted, without increasing its length, to avoid the interior of C. Thus such a cycle can effectively
make the interior irrelevant with respect to certain connections. In intersection graphs, the rerouting
may incur additional cost, invalidating this argument. These additional costs have a cascading effect on
the algorithm: they require extra layers of proof ideas to make certain steps more robust to such cost
increases, which in turn require further extra layers of arguments in the later parts of the algorithm.
1.2
Organization
The paper is organized as follows. Section 2 gives an overview of both previous techniques in planar
graphs and our new techniques in intersection graphs to achieve the desired EPTASes. In Section 3,
we recall standard notation for graphs and geometric objects, and define new concepts introduced in
the paper, such as wireframes and object frames.
Sections 4–7 contain basic results that are crucial for the algorithms. As they are mostly used
in black-box fashion, the reader may decide to only skim these sections at first, and jump directly
to the algorithmic results starting in Section 8. In more detail, Section 4 shows how a set of objects
can be turned into an object frame while providing the same connectivity for terminal points. This
result is crucial for the structural results for Steiner tree (Section 10), but a special case with only two
terminals is used already in the construction of the so-called skeleton and the spanner in Section 8.
Section 5 presents our results on Lipschitz embeddings into planar graphs. These results are used,
among other places, in Section 6, where we prove our contraction decomposition results for geometric
objects. Section 7 contains the sparsification results for both Subset TSP and Steiner Tree, showing
that the clique size can be reduced to a function of 1/ε.
Our main result for Subset TSP (Theorem 1.4) is proved in Section 8, by first constructing the subset
spanner and then using it with contraction decomposition and a bounded-treewidth algorithm.
Theorem 1.5 for Steiner Tree is proved in Sections 9 and 10: the so-called mortar graph is con-
structed in Section 9 and the main structural theorem for Steiner trees is stated in Section 10. The
faster PTAS for Steiner Tree (Theorem 1.6) is proved in Section 11 using this structural understanding.
Section 12 proves our APX-hardness results (Theorem 1.7), as well as the connection to planar
problems (Theorem 1.8).
Most of our results are proved for so-called α-standard intersection graphs of polygons. Ap-
pendix A discusses how intersection graphs of similarly sized connected fat objects can be converted
5


## Page 9


to such a representation.
2
Overview
2.1
EPTAS for Subset TSP
We first review the main steps of the proof of Theorem 1.1 by Klein [68], and then briefly explain the
new ideas and concepts in our algorithm for the geometric intersection graph setting. In this and the
following sections, we first go through the main algorithmic components of the previous algorithms
and then introduce the new components that we developed.
2.1.1
Overview of Klein’s techniques [68] in planar graphs
Skeleton and subset spanner construction
The first step in the algorithm of Klein [68] is to build
a subset spanner: as stated in the first part of Theorem 1.1, this is a subgraph G′, containing all the termi-
nals, such that |V (G′)| ≤poly(1/ε)SubsetTSP(G, T) and distG′(x, y) ≤(1 + ε)distG(x, y) for any
x, y ∈T. Let us start with any tree containing every vertex of T and having size O(SubsetTSP(G, T));
for example, a constant-factor approximate Steiner tree is suitable. This is likely not a correct subset
spanner yet: a shortest x −y path P may use “shortcuts” outside the tree. To obtain a spanner, we
need to extend the tree (with a factor of poly(1/ε) increase in total size) in a way that the shortest path
P can be rerouted (at the cost of at most a factor of 1 + ε increase in length). It is convenient for the
description of the algorithm to change the setting: using the standard step of “cutting open the tree”,
we can assume that the boundary cycle C of the infinite face contains all of T, and we want to extend
C into a subset spanner, increasing its size by at most a factor of poly(1/ε).
As an intermediate step, we extend C into a so-called skeleton (a subgraph of G), satisfying a
weaker property. Let F be a face of the skeleton; with slight abuse of notation, let G[F] be the subgraph
of G containing every edge inside F or on the boundary of F. The boundary of each (finite) face F of
the skeleton is divided into two paths, North and South, such that
• For any two vertices x, y of North, the subpath North[x, y] is a shortest x −y path in G[F].
• For any two vertices x, y of South, the subpath South[x, y] is a (1+ε)-approximate shortest x−y
path in G[F].
We extend the cycle C with the following iterative procedure. Suppose that C has two vertices x, y
such that there is a “shortcut”: an x −y path P inside C such that |P| is more than a factor of (1 + ε)
shorter than the x −y subpath P ′ of C. Let us choose P such that |P ′| is minimum possible. If we
extend C by adding P to it, then we are creating two faces (see Figure 2):
• A face bounded by P and P ′, which satisfies the requirements when we define P to be North
(as it is a shortest path) and P ′ to be South (where (1 + ε)-approximate minimality follows from
the minimal choice of P ′).
• A face bounded by the cycle C′, which is obtained from C by replacing P with P ′.
Let us continue this process with the cycle C′, stopping when no shortcut can be found in the cycle.
A charging argument shows that the total length of all the added paths is O(1/ε) times the length of
the initial cycle C.
Does the resulting graph have the subset spanner property? Consider a shortest x −y path P in
G and consider a subpath P ′ that intersects the skeleton only in the endpoints x′ and y′, i.e., P ′ goes
inside some face F (see Figure 2(iii)). If x′ and y′ are both on the North part of F, then the shortest
path property shows that P ′ can be rerouted along North without any increase of length. Similarly,
if x′ and y′ are both on South, then P ′ can be rerouted along South with a (1 + ε) factor increase of
length. To handle the situation where P ′ connects North and South, Klein [68] constructed a spanner
connecting two paths, in the following sense:

Lemma 2.1 (Bipartite spanner, Klein [68]). Let G be a graph, and let Q be a shortest path in G
from a to b, and let Q′ be an arbitrary path from a to b. Then for any ε > 0 there is a subgraph H
with |H| = O(1/ε3)|Q′| such that for any q ∈V (Q) and q′ ∈V (Q′) we have distH∪Q∪Q′(q, q′) ⩽
(1 + ε)distG(q, q′).
Using Lemma 2.1 in every face of the skeleton with Q = North and Q′ = South allows us to extend
the skeleton such that P ′ can be rerouted with a factor of 1 + ε increase whenever it connects North
and South. Overall, we create the required spanner having size O(1/ε4|V (C)|).
Contraction decomposition and bounded-treewidth algorithms
Using the subset spanner con-
struction, we can assume that |V (G)|, |E(G)| ≤poly(1/ε)SubsetTSP(G, T). Next we would like
to decrease the treewidth of G by contracting an appropriate small set of edges. To find this set of
edges, we use the following contraction decomposition result (a similar result was implicitely proved
by Klein [69]).
Theorem 2.2 (Contraction Decomposition for Planar Graphs, Demaine et al. [51]). For any
integer k ⩾2, the edge set of any planar graph G can be partitioned into k sets E1, . . . , Ek such that
tw(G/Ei) = O(k) for all i ∈[k]. The partition can be found in O(n3/2 log n) time.
The smallest set Ei has size at most |E(G)|/k = poly(1/ε)SubsetTSP(G, T)/k. Thus by setting
k to be sufficiently large (but still polynomial in 1/ε), we can ensure that |Ei| ≤ε · SubsetTSP(G, T).
It can be shown that contracting a set E of edges changes the optimum of Subset TSP by at most
2|E|, hence it is sufficient to approximate SubsetTSP(G/Ei, T), where we know that tw(G/Ei) =
O(k) = poly(1/ε). Such an instance can be solved in time 2poly(1/ε)·nO(1) using standard dynamic-
programming techniques.
2.1.2
Solving Subset TSP in geometric intersection graphs
Cell partition
Intersection graphs can contain large cliques, making their structure significantly
more complicated compared to planar graphs. However, it is well-known that in case of similarly
sized fat objects, the intersection graph G can be partitioned into cliques such that each clique is
adjacent to a bounded number of other cliques. For the description of this partition, it is convenient to
define similarly sized fat objects differently. We say that an intersection graph of simple polygons is
α-standard if (i) each component of the intersection of two polygons is a simple polygon, (ii) no three
polygon edges meet at one point, (iii) each polygon contains a closed disk of radius 1 and has diameter
at most α. We denote by Iα the set of all α-standard intersection graphs. It can be shown that an
intersection graph of δ-similarly sized β-fat polygons is in Iα for some α depending on β and δ (see
Theorem 3.4). From now on, we state the results for this class Iα.
7


## Page 11


Formally, we define the cell partition P as follows. Consider the grid of points in the plane with
integer coordinates. By item (iii) in the definition of α-standard, every object has one such point in its
interior. Let us assign each object to the lexicographically first grid point that is in its interior; let P
be the partition of the objects according to which point they are assigned to. Clearly, every class of P
induces a clique in G. We denote by GP the graph obtained by contracting each class of P into a single
vertex. Often, it is usefully to think of the structure of G in terms of the graph GP, as this graph better
reveals its close-to-planar structure. Observe that two objects can intersect only if they are assigned
to lattice points at distance at most O(α). Thus a vertex of GP can be adjacent only to vertices that
represent points at distance O(α) in the plane; in particular GP has maximum degree O(α2).
(NEW) Sparsification
A clique in the partition P can be large, but in Section 7 we show that the
instance can be preprocessed to make the size of these cliques bounded. First, if a clique contains
many terminals, then (as they all have to be visited anyways), we may assume that they are mostly
visited after each other. This observation allows us to decrease the number of terminals and assume
that |C ∩T| = O(1/ε) for every C ∈P. Let us select a distinguished vertex of each clique. A
solution of Subset TSP consists of paths between terminals. If such a path is longer than O(1/ε), then
we can modify it in such a way that after every O(1/ε) steps it visits a distinguished vertex, and this
modification increases the cost only by a factor of 1 + O(ε). This means that we can assume that this
(1 + ε)-approximate solution consists of paths of length O(1/ε) between terminals and distinguished
vertices. Let us fix such a path for every pair. Consider a clique C ∈P. There are O(1/ε2) other
cliques that are at distance O(1/ε), thus there are O(1/ε3) terminals and distinguished vertices at
distance O(1/ε). This means that there are at most O(1/ε6) pairs of these vertices such that the fixed
shortest path of length O(1/ε) between them go through C. As a shortest path can use only at most
two vertices of a clique, this means that only O(1/ε6) vertices of the clique are used by these paths
and the rest of the vertices can be removed from the graph without affecting the (1 + ε)-approximate
solution. Therefore, we can assume from now on that every clique has size poly(1/ε).
(NEW) Wireframes and regions
Our goal is again to find a subset spanner, as required by the
first part of Theorem 1.4. In the planar case, the faces of the skeleton partitioned the instance into
(internally) disjoint regions, and we could handle each region independently. The first problem we
encounter in the case of intersection graphs is that the notion of faces, regions, being inside, being
outside, etc. are much less clear. To tackle this difficulty, we introduce a formalism that turns out to be
essential for the robust and formal description of the algorithms (especially for Steiner Tree).
Given an intersection graph G, a wireframe is a plane graph eH where vertices are points in the
plane and edges are polygonal curves. Each vertex v of eH has a parent object p(v) of G such that v is
located in the object p(v). Moreover, for each edge uv of eH, the corresponding polygonal curve can
be split into a part inside p(u) and a part inside p(v) (see Definition 3.6 for the precise definition). The
graph eH can have much fewer edges and vertices than G: it can be thought of as a planar subgraph
of the intersection graph, with a certain form of planar representation. Note, however, that it is not
necessarily a subgraph, as multiple points can have the same parent object.
(NEW) Skeleton and subset spanner for intersection graphs.
In Section 8, similarly to the planar
algorithm, we first construct a 2-connected skeleton. In light of the sparsification result mentioned
above, it is sufficient to bound by poly(1/ε) · SubsetTSP(G, T) the number of cliques of the cell
partition P that appear in the skeleton, as this also bounds the number of objects and hence proves
Theorem 1.4(1).
Formally, now this skeleton is a wireframe of the original intersection graph G. As before, we
want to divide the boundary of each face into North and South such that any subpath of them is
(approximately) shorter than any path connecting the endpoints through objects “inside” the face. We
use the following definition to remove any potential ambiguity of what it means that a path of objects
is inside the face. Let R be a region of the plane corresponding to a face of the skeleton. We define the
8


## Page 12


intersection graph G|R, where for every object v ∈V (G), we introduce into G|R every component
of v ∩R as a separate object (so the objects in G|R are also connected polygons). Note that, due to
these smaller objects created by the intersection with the boundary, the polygons in G|R are no longer
similarly sized fat objects. When constructing the skeleton, we want to ensure that the boundary of
each face is divided into two parts, North and South, such that between any two vertices x, y on North,
the subpath of North is not longer than the distance between x and y in G|R (and similarly for South
with a factor of 1 + ε).
We can build the skeleton with a similar iterative procedure as in the planar case. There is one
technical challenge that appears when adding the shortcut and moving from a larger region R to a
smaller region R′: because we are cutting the objects at the boundary, the total number/complexity of
the polygons in G|R′ may be larger than in G|R. Thus repeated applications of this step may lead to
an exponential number of objects. We overcome this issue by an appropriate (nontrivial) choice of the
polygonal curve that we are cutting along.
After constructing the skeleton, for every face bounding some region R, we use Theorem 2.1 on
G|R to add connections between North and South (note that Theorem 2.1 works in arbitrary graphs).
Does the resulting set of objects have the spanning property? Consider a path P between two terminals
and let P ′ be a subpath that enters and leaves region R by crossing North of R. Here is the point where
the issue about crossing paths demonstrated in Figure 1 comes into play: it is possible that the subpath
P ′ is not using any object of the path North. This means that if we want to use a subpath of North for
rerouting a path, then the length is increased by one when we “jump” to North, and then once more
when we “jump” back to the original path. Therefore, even though North has the required shortest
path property, rerouting P ′ on North may increase length by 2. If P ′ is of length Ω(1/ε), this increase
by 2 is introduces only 1 + O(ε) multiplicative error, and hence tolerable. However, if |P ′| is o(1/ε),
then the increase by 2 is significant.
Let us notice that if |P ′| = O(1/ε), then P ′ is fully contained in the distance-O(1/ε) neighborhood
of the skeleton. Thus if we include in the subset spanner the distance-O(1/ε) neighborhood of the
skeleton, then every such short path P ′ is fully included in the subset spanner. Consequently, if the
subpath P ′ leaves the subset spanner, then it has to be of length Ω(1/ε), hence it can be rerouted along
North with a factor 1 + O(ε) increase. We can argue similarly for subpaths entering and leaving by
crossing South, or for subpaths connecting North and South. Notice that there are O(1/ε2) cliques in
the cell partition that are at distance at most O(1/ε) from an object. Thus adding the distance-O(1/ε)
neighborhood of the skeleton increases the number of cells only by a factor of poly(1/ε) and hence we
can ensure that the obtained subset spanner is the union of poly(1/ε) · SubsetTSP(G, T) cliques of
the cell partition, as we wanted. Moreover, our earlier sparsification ensured that each clique contains
poly(1/ε) objects, hence in fact the subset spanner consists of poly(1/ε) · SubsetTSP(G, T) objects,
proving the first part of Theorem 1.4.
(NEW) Contraction decomposition via Lipschitz embedding
Having constructed the subset
spanner, we can assume that |V (G)| ≤poly(1/ε)SubsetTSP(G, T). To reduce the problem to an
instance with bounded treewidth, we would like to use an analog of Theorem 2.2 for intersection
graphs. Let us observe that the intersection graph itself can be a clique, thus it is impossible to re-
duce treewidth to a constant by contracting a small part of the graph. However, by the sparsification
procedure described above, we can assume that there is a cell partition where every clique has size
poly(1/ε). Therefore, in Section 6, we prove the analog of Theorem 2.2 under a bound on the clique
size. As a minor notational difference, instead of edge contractions, it will be convenient to consider
contracting vertex sets. Given a set X of vertices, we let G X = G/E(G[X]) to be the graph ob-
tained by contracting each component of G[X] into a single vertex. The following statement is proved
by Theorem 6.1(ii):
Theorem 2.3. Let G ∈Iα be an intersection graph of similarly sized fat objects with cell partition P
where each clique C ∈P has size at most |C| ⩽ℓ. Given an integer k, we can compute in polynomial
time k sets X1, . . . , Xk of vertices such that Pk
i=1 |Xi| = O(|P|ℓ) and tw(G Xi) = O(kℓ).
9


## Page 13


We remark that in case of unit disk graphs, there are now known contraction decomposition theo-
rems [10,74] that can handle cliques, but due to our more general objects these results are insufficient
for our purposes. (In fact, they are insufficient for our Steiner tree algorithm even if the input graph is
a unit disk graph, as we need to modify our starting objects via region restrictions and other changes.)
With Theorem 2.3 at hand and using the bounds on |P| and on the maximum clique size, we can
solve the instance in time 2poly(1/ε) · nO(1) using a standard dynamic programming algorithm solving
the problem on bounded-treewidth graphs.
It is natural to approach the proof of Theorem 2.3 via the graph GP: we would like to find sets of
vertices X′
i in GP such that GP X′
i has treewidth O(k). For each X′
i, there is a set Xi of at most |X′
i|ℓ
vertices in G, corresponding to the cliques in X′
i. Furthermore, if we replace each vertex of GP X′
i by
a clique of size ℓ, then we get a supergraph of G Xi. Thus the treewidth bound of O(k) on GP X′
i
would imply the required bound of O(kℓ) on G Xi.
How can we find these sets X′
i in GP? As GP is somewhat close to being planar, we can try to
approximate it with a genuinely planar graph H and then invoke Theorem 2.2 on H. However, the
requirements of this approximation is fairly delicate and naive ideas (such as representing GP by an
induced subgraph of a sufficiently fine grid) fail. The reason is that H has to strike a balance between
two conflicting requirements:
• H should not have too many edges: otherwise, contracting a small set in H does not correspond
to the contraction of a small set in GP.
• H should not have too few edges: otherwise, small treewidth after contracting a set in H does
not mean that contracting the corresponding set in GP results in small treewidth.
It turns out that the notion of Lipschitz embedding satisfies these requirements. We say that a mapping
f : V (G) →V (H) is c-Lipschitz for some constant c ⩾1 if for any pair of vertices x, y ∈V (G) we
have that
1
c · distG(x, y) ⩽distH(f(x), f(y)) ⩽c · distG(x, y).
For unit disk intersection graphs, a construction of a c-Lipschitz embedding into a planar graph is
known for some constant c: in fact, they admit planar c-spanners [19, 28]. In Section 5, we construct
c-Lipschitz embeddings for the intersection graphs of connected similarly sized fat objects. The fact
that we are handling non-convex objects makes the construction of the embedding significantly more
challenging and requires a different strategy. (See Theorem 5.2 for a more detailed theorem statement.)
Theorem 2.4. For any α > 0, there exists a positive constant c = O(α32) such that for any G ∈Iα given
by its representation the following hold. Let P be the cell partition of G. Then in polynomial time one can
construct a plane graph H with the same vertex set as GP such that the identity mapping is c-Lipschitz
from GP to H.
Very briefly, we want to represent edges of GP by polygonal curves that are covered by two ad-
jacent objects. Then we introduce a new vertex at each point where two such curves intersect each
other. If the objects are convex, then the curves can be chosen to be simple and it is easy to bound
the number of these intersections. However, if the objects are not convex, then already two curves
can have lots of intersections. In case two curves intersect multiple times, we can try to reroute one
curve on the other to reduce the number of intersections, at the cost of making some curves longer.
However, doing this iteratively can make curves arbitrarily longer. In order to avoid this, we split the
curves into a constant number of nonconflicting sets, perform many reroutings in parallel, and then
repeat this in a constant number of rounds. Theorem 2.4 requires that H and GP have the same ver-
tex set, but our construction introduced many additional vertices at the intersections. To get rid of
these intersection vertices, we can contract the Voronoi regions of the original vertices, retaining the
Lipschitz propeperty and ensuring that the planar graph H has the same vertex set as GP.
10


## Page 14


The constructed planar graph H will be used as a proxy for GP when trying to reduce treewidth
using contractions. We invoke Theorem 2.2 to find a small set X in H whose contraction reduces
treewidth. To complete the proof of Theorem 2.3, we need to argue that an analogous contraction
reduces treewidth in GP as well. This requires a delicate argument that modifies a tree decomposition
of a contraction of H to obtain a tree decomposition of a contraction of GP. In particular, it is not
sufficient to contract X in GP, but we need to contract a small neighborhood of X.
Lemma 2.5. Let G and H be graphs with the same vertex set V , so that the identity map of V is c-
Lipschitz from G to H. Let X ⊂V be arbitrary, and let Xr be the set of vertices whose endpoints have
H-distance at most r from some vertex in X, i.e., we set Xr = NH(X, r). Assume moreover that H has
degree at most ∆. Then tw(G Xc2) = O(∆c) · tw(H X).
2.2
EPTAS for Steiner Tree
2.2.1
Overview of the techniques of Borradaile et al. [24] in planar graphs
Let us review the EPTAS of Borradaile, Klein, and Mathieu [24] for Steiner Tree on planar graphs and
then explain what new algorithmic components are needed in case of intersection graphs (for example,
techniques to obtain noncrossing structures of various types, which can be taken for granted in the case
of planar graphs). The algorithm for planar graphs starts with the same idea as in the case of Subset
TSP: constructing a Steiner-tree spanner,1 that is, a subgraph that admits a (1 + ε)-optimal Steiner tree
of the terminals in T. However, the size of the Steiner-tree spanner obtained by the algorithm can
be bounded only by 2poly(1/ε) · StTree(G, T), hence the contraction decomposition can reduce the
treewidth only to 2poly(1/ε), resulting in 22poly(1/ε) · nO(1) running time after performing contraction
decomposition and solving the resulting bounded-treewidth instance.
Columns and the mortar graphs
The algorithm for Steiner Tree starts similarly to the Subset TSP
algorithm: with the construction of the skeleton of size poly(1/ε)StTree(G, T). But then each face F
is further partitioned, the following way. Consider a face of the skeleton, with its boundary partitioned
into South and North. Let s0 be an endpoint of South and then for i = 1, 2, . . . , vertex si is defined
to be the first vertex si on South such that the distance from si to North inside the face F is at most
|South[si−1, si]|/ε. Let column Ci be a shortest path from si to North. In a planar graph, we can assume
that the columns do not cross. It is easy to observe that the total length of the columns in a face is at
most |South|/ε. Thus the total length of all columns in all faces of the skeleton is at most O(1/ε) times
the total size of the skeleton, hence it is also poly(1/ε) · StTree(G, T).
We fix a constant κ = poly(1/ε), and in each face of the skeleton we extend the skeleton by every
κth column. These columns partition each face into multiple bricks, and the columns form the East and
West sides of these bricks (see Figure 3). The skeleton extended by these columns is called the mortar
graph.
More precisely, we select an i ∈[κ] and, starting with the ith column, we include every κth column
in the mortar graph . By an averaging argument, there is an i such that the total length of the added
columns is at most a 1/κ fraction of the total length of all columns. If κ is sufficiently large, then
the total length is O(ε · StTree(G, T)). Therefore, we can afford to “buy” these columns and can be
assumed to be part of the solution. This can be expressed by contracting them, thus they will not play
much role in the later arguments. The purpose of extending the skeleton this way is that now we can
assume that the sequence s1, s2, . . . has length at most κ in each brick. This structural property is
heavily exploited in the next step.
Simplifying a forest
We can think of the solution as having two parts: a subset of the mortar graph
and a forest inside each face of the mortar graph. If a tree in a brick is attached only to the North or
South side, then it can be replaced by a subpath of that side, increasing cost only by at most a 1 + ε

Figure 4: The graph modification that restricts interactions between bricks to the portals.
factor. But, in general, a tree can connect several vertices of the North side with several vertices of the
South side, and then it is not clear if the shortest path properties of North and South can be exploited for
any simplification. Nevertheless, Borradaile, Klein, and Mathieu [24, Thm. 10.7] showed by a careful
simplification argument that parts of the tree can be replaced by subpaths of the boundary in such a
way that the size of the solution increases only by at most a factor of (1 + ε) and the forest in each
brick is joined to the boundary of the brick only at poly(1/ε) vertices.
Portals
We want to ensure that the inside of a brick has limited and well controlled interaction with
the boundary, that is, the solution enters the inside of the brick via a limited number of portals. Let
us consider a brick B an let us designate θ = poly(1/ε) portals on the boundary of B at roughly
equal distance from each other. That is, if ℓ(B) is the length of the boundary of B, then the portals
are at distance roughly ℓ(B)/θ from each other. Whenever the solution has a tree inside a brick that
is attached to a vertex v of the boundary, then let us extend the solution by connecting v to the closest
portal. For each such vertex v, this modification increases the cost by at most ℓ(B)/θ. As we have
assumed that the forest inside B is attached to the boundary at poly(1/ε) vertices, the total increase
incurred by brick B is at most ℓ(B) · poly(1/ε)/θ. As the total size of the mortar graph created so far
was poly(1/ε)·StTree(G, T), the total increase of length is poly(1/ε)·StTree(G, T)/θ when summed
over every brick B. If θ = poly(1/ε) is sufficiently large, then this increase is ε · StTree(G, T). Thus
after some slight modification of the graph G (as demonstrated in Figure 4), we can assume that the
inside of each brick is connected only to the θ portals on the boundary.
Constructing the Steiner-tree spanner
Inside each brick B, the only role of the solution is to
connect certain subsets of the θ portals on the boundary of B. We need to ensure that the this can
be done as efficiently in the Steiner-tree spanner as in the original graph. For this purpose, for every
subset of the θ portals of B, we compute an optimal Steiner tree in B and add this tree to the Steiner-
tree spanner. Note that an optimal Steiner tree with at most θ terminals can be computed in time
2O(θ) · nO(1) = 2poly(1/ε) · nO(1) in any graph. Furthermore, in our case we can exploit that each
brick is planar and the terminals are on the boundary, in which case the problem is polynomial-time
12


## Page 16


solvable [55]. Clearly, the size of each such tree is at most the length ℓ(B) of the boundary of B, as the
boundary clearly connects all these terminals. Thus the total length we add for each brick B is at most
2θ · ℓ(B) = 2poly(1/ε) · ℓ(B). Summing for every brick B, this increases the total length of the mortar
graph by a factor of 2poly(1/ε), resulting in a Steiner-tree spanner of length 2poly(1/ε)StTree(G, T).
2.2.2
Constructing a Steiner tree spanner in geometric intersection graphs
(NEW) Noncrossing columns via Lipschitz embedding
Section 9 is devoted to constructing an
analog of the mortar graph in the intersection graph setting. In the case of intersection graphs, if we
define the columns as shortest paths from each si to North, then these paths may cross and therefore
they are not suitable for defining the East/West sides of the brick. One could try to uncross these paths,
but in intersection graphs jumping from one path to the other can increase the length, and it is hard to
control the total increase of length after iteratively uncrossing paths. To overcome this issue in a robust
way, we use a Lipschitz embedding of the union of the columns into a planar graph (more precisely,
into a wireframe of the union of the columns). Using this embedding, we can find shortest paths that
correspond to noncrossing curves in the wireframe, hence they can be used as the East and West sides
of the brick. The Lipschitz property of the embedding ensures that the columns defined this way have
the required length and distance properties.
There is a technical complication due to the fact that we have Lipschitz-embedding results only for
similarly sized fat objects. However, the objects in G|R can be non-fat and have smaller diameter, as
G|R contains objects intersected with the boundary of R. We solve this issue by considering the union
of the first few objects in each column as a single object, ensuring that at least one of these objects
is disjoint from the boundary of R (and similarly for the last few objects). Note that the union of a
constant number of similarly sized fat objects changes diameter and fatness only by a constant factor,
thus we can still consider this set of objects as similarly sized fat. A further technical complication that
appears here is that the created objects can have holes. However, we can modify them by “puncturing
the holes” in a way that the intersection graph does not change.
Another technical difference compared to the case of planar graphs is that we have to be more
careful about the columns going on or close to the boundary of the region. It is even possible that
multiple columns overlap with the northern or southern boundary. In the clean world of planar graphs,
such shared subpaths are inconsequential. On the other hand, for intersection graphs, handling this
issue introduces additional technicalities. In particular, we need to allow that on some part of the
boundary South and East overlap, complicating the definitions considerably.
(NEW) Sparsification
In Section 7, we show that, similarly to Subset TSP, large cliques can be
reduced in size with a factor of at most (1 + ε) change in the optimum value. However, for Steiner
Tree, we can guarantee only an exponential 2poly(1/ε) upper bound on the reduced clique size. The
crucial difference is that the role of a vertex in a clique is not just to be part of a path of length O(1/ε)
connecting two vertices at distance at most O(1/ε), but to be part of a subtree connecting a subset of
up to O(1/ε) vertices at distance at most O(1/ε). There are exponentially many such subsets and for
each subset potentially a different vertex of the clique is needed, resulting in the exponential upper
bound.
(NEW) Uncrossing trees and object frames
In Section 10, we would like to use the forest simpli-
fication result [24, Thm. 10.7] from the planar algorithm as a black box. This seems reasonable: even
though intersection graphs can be more complicated than planar graphs, the solution inside a brick is
still just a forest. The forest simplification result considers only forests, so one could argue that it is
irrelevant whether this forest is taken from a planar graph or from an intersection graph. However, it
is more precise to say that the simplification is done on a planar graph that consists of a boundary cycle
and a forest inside it; then the simplification argument replaces some parts of the forest with subpaths
of the boundary. Therefore, in order to invoke this result, we need to define such a planar graph based
13

(i)
(ii)
Figure 5: (i) The intersection graph of the 15 red objects and the 3+3 objects on the boundary is a tree
(shown by blue). However, the union of this tree and the green boundary is nonplanar, as it contains
the subdivision of a K3,3. (ii) Replacing the 15 red objects with an object frame of 18 subobjects. Union
of the intersection graph of these objects and the boundary is now planar.
on the solution in the intersection graph. The problem is that even if an intersection graph is a tree,
the objects can cross each other and hence it might not be possible to draw the tree without crossing
edges in the plane if we want to respect respect the topology of the original objects on the boundary
(see Figure 5(i)). Thus the union of the forest and the boundary can be highly nonplanar.
In order to be able to use [24, Thm. 10.7], we create a “really planar” representation of the forest
inside the brick. An object frame of an intersection graph G of polygons is an intersection graph H,
where each object v of H is a subpolygon of a parent object p(v) of G and the polygons of H are
internally disjoint (plus some further nondegeneracy conditions). Note that p(u) = p(v) is possible
for u, v ∈V (H), that is, the object frame can use multiple subpolygons of a polygon. We show in
Section 4 (Theorem 4.2) that, given an intersection graph G that covers a set of t points in the plane,
we can construct an object frame H of G such that (i) H is a forest, (ii) |V (H)| ≤|V (G)| + O(t), (iii)
H provides the same connectivity of the points as G, that is, if two of the t points are covered by a
component of G, then it is also connected by a component of H. With this result at hand, we replace
the forest of the solution inside a brick by an object frame (see Figure 5(ii)). Now the forest defined
by this object frame together with the boundary form a planar graph, hence [24, Thm. 10.7] can be
invoked.
(NEW) Solution generators
In the case of planar graphs, we considered the solution to be com-
posed of two disjoint parts: a subgraph of the mortar graph and forests inside the bricks. However,
unlike in planar graphs, it is not straightforward to modify the instance such that the interior of a brick
is connected only to the portals of the boundary. Moreover, the situation is even more complicated: as
it was the case for Subset TSP, we need to include in the Steiner-tree spanner the distance-poly(1/ε)
neighborhood of the mortar graph, making it even less clear how the interior is supposed to be con-
nected only to the portals.
For intersection graphs, we need to adopt a slightly different viewpoint. Instead of saying that
the solution is partitioned into a subgraph of the mortar graph and forests, we say that the solution
provides two things: a subgraph S0 in the neighborhood of the mortar graph and connections between
some subsets of the portals. Of course, the connections between the portals are realized by trees and
these trees have to go through the neighborhood of the mortar graph, but we do not care what exactly
these trees do there. In particular, we do not care if these trees use objects that are already in S0: we can
achieve (1 + ε)-approximate solution size even if we are double counting such objects. In Section 10,
we prove our main structural result for Steiner Tree, which shows that there is an (1+ε)-approximate
solution that arises this way. Proving this requires modifying a solution in a way that components that
connect portals are not needed to connect other parts of the boundary.
14


## Page 18


For the robust handling of the solutions inside bricks, we introduce a more abstract formalism,
which will be particularly useful for the next result, the faster PTAS (Theorem 1.6). A portal-respecting
generator consists of a subset S0 of the distance-poly(1/ε) neighborhood of the mortar graph and
for each brick B a collection F of subsets of portals. The generator is feasible if S0 connects all the
terminals in T if we extend S0 with “virtual edges” connecting, for every P ∈F, the vertices of P to
each other. Intuitively, a generator represents solutions that consist of S0 and for each set P of portals
in F, an arbitrary tree connecting P. If the generator is feasible, then any set obtained this way is
indeed a solution. We define the cost of the generator to be |S0|, plus, for every P ∈F, the cost of the
minimum Steiner tree connecting P. Clearly, any solution represented by a feasible generator has size
at most the cost of the generator. Our main structural result can be formulated as saying that there is
a feasible generator whose cost is at most (1 + ε)StTree(G, T).
2.3
Faster PTAS for Steiner Tree
Borradaile, Klein, and Mathieu [24] presented a PTAS for planar graphs that, instead of using the
Steiner-tree spanner, uses dynamic programming on the mortar graph (Theorem 1.3). Our proof of
Theorem 1.6 uses a very different approach. Therefore, instead of giving an overview of the technique
of Borradaile, Klein, and Mathieu [24], we first sketch a different, more streamlined algorithm for
planar graphs. The ideas in this sketch form the basis for an analogous algorithm for intersection
graphs presented in Section 11. However, the difficulties arising in the intersection graph setting lead
to major technical challenges and makes it necessary to substantially redesign this approach.
(NEW) PTAS via weighted tree-representative vertices in planar graphs
Using contraction
decomposition, we can find a set X of O(ε · StTree(G, T)) vertices whose contraction reduces the
treewidth of the mortar graph M to poly(1/ε). We can afford to include these vertices into the so-
lution, which is effectively the same as contracting them in the input. Thus we may assume that the
mortar graph has treewidth poly(1/ε). This of course does not mean that the input graph has bounded
treewidth: the parts of the graph inside the bricks can still be complicated. However, this part of the
graph is used only for trees connecting terminals; the idea is to represent each possible tree with a
single vertex, making what appears inside each brick bounded.
It is convenient to switch at this point to a vertex-weighted problem, where each vertex v has a
nonnegative weight w(v) and the task is to minimize the total weight of the solution. (Note that we
are not aiming to solve the vertex-weighted version in general, but the specific instance appearing in
the rest of the algorithm can be conveniently described and solved as a weighted instance.) We extend
the mortar graph M the following way. Consider the θ = poly(1/ε) portals of a brick B and for
any subset P of them, introduce a vertex vP that is adjacent to every portal in P; let the weight of
vP be the size of the minimum Steiner tree containing P. We repeat this step for every brick B and
every subset of the portals of P, this way we are introducing at most 2θ = 2poly(1/ε) new vertices for
each brick B (note that the resulting graph M∗can be highly nonplanar). It is clear that any solution
of weight W of this new weighted instance (M∗, T) can be turned into a solution of size W of the
original unweighted instance (G, T). Also, it should be clear that if the original instance (G, T) has a
solution of size W that consists of a subgraph of the mortar graph M and trees connecting subsets of
portals in the bricks, then the new instance (M∗, T) has a solution of weight W. Thus it is sufficient
to solve this new, weighted instance (M∗, T).
How can we bound the treewidth of M∗? It is known that introducing a new vertex into each
face of a planar graph and connecting it arbitrarily with vertices of the face changes treewidth only
by a constant factor. We are introducing at most 2poly(1/ε) vertices per face, which then increases
treewidth by at most a factor of 2poly(1/ε). To see this treewidth bound, let us first introduce a vertex
vF in each face F such that vF is adjacent to every vertex of F, and consider a tree decomposition of
this graph. Whenever vF appears in a bag, let us replace it with a block of 2poly(1/ε) copies. It is clear
that the resulting tree decomposition has treewidth at most O(2poly(1/ε)) times the original treewidth.
15

Figure 6: (i) Even if a subgraph (green) has constant treewidth, its neighborhood can induce a a graph of
arbitrary large treewidth. (ii) Extending a single brick of the mortar graph with its distance-poly(1/ε)
neighborhood (gray shaded region) can create multiple “islands”.
The standard dynamic programming algorithm for Steiner Tree can be easily adapted to the vertex-
weighted version. However, as this algorithm is exponential in treewidth, this would still give only a
22poly(1/ε) · nO(1) time algorithm, which is weaker than our goal.
To improve the running time, let us observe that the dynamic programming can be made faster on
such decompositions. In each bag of the decomposition, we have at most poly(1/ε) original vertices
of M, plus at most poly(1/ε) blocks of tree-representative vertices, with each block having size at
most 2poly(1/ε). The important observation is that if a solution for the original instance (G, T) uses
at most θ = poly(1/ε) trees in each brick, then there is a corresponding solution of the new instance
(M∗, T) that uses at most poly(1/ε) tree-representative vertices from each block. Therefore, there are
(poly(1/ε)·2poly(1/ε))poly(1/ε) = 2poly(1/ε) different subsets of a bag that can potentially appear in such
a solution and we can restrict our search to solutions with this property. The running time of (weighted)
Steiner tree mainly depends on the number of subproblems defined in the dynamic programming,
which in turn mainly depends on the number of possible subsets of vertices that need to be considered
at each bag. Given our bound on the number of relevant subsets, it follows that standard dynamic
programming can solve the problem in 2poly(1/ε) · nO(1) time.
(NEW) Contracting through bricks
If we try to follow the same strategy to speed up our PTAS
for intersection graphs, then the main source of the difficulty is that the solution is not composed
from a subgraph of the mortar graph plus trees in the bricks, but from a subgraph of the distance-
poly(1/ε) neighborhood of the mortar graph plus trees in the bricks. Let us observe that even if
the mortar graph has bounded treewidth, it very well may be that the neighborhood of the mortar
graph has large treewidth (see Figure 6(i) for an example). Inside a brick, if we consider a distance-
poly(1/ε) neighborhood of the boundary, then this may create connections between different parts
of the boundary and can split the inside of the brick into multiple “islands”. By their construction,
the bricks have lots of structural properties and we may try to argue that including the neighborhood
cannot create so many extra connections to increase treewidth significantly. While it may be possible
to prove this, we found potential arguments very tedious and therefore chose a different strategy.
In Section 11, instead of using contraction decomposition to find a set whose contraction decreases
the treewidth of the mortar graph, we use contraction decomposition on the distance-poly(1/ε) neigh-
borhood of the mortar graph to reduce the treewidth of this neighborhood to poly(1/ε). Note that now
the set X we contract can go through a brick (see Figure 7). Therefore, it is difficult to determine where
we should put a tree-representative vertex corresponding to a brick. Instead, we put tree-representative
vertices in each island. As each island is in a single brick, these vertices should represent a part of one
of the 2poly(1/ε) possible trees that can appear in this brick. (Here it is a technical complication that
the restriction of a tree to an island can consist of many components.)
If the graph were planar, then the scheme described in the previous paragraph could be made to

work, with techniques similar to what we described in the planar case. In the case of intersection
graphs, the set X for contraction needs to be found in a planar constant-Lipschitz representation of
the graph (or more precisely, of the cell partition). Moreover, we need to insert the tree-representative
vertices into the faces of this planar representation. In the end, we argue that certain properties (such as
treewidth) of this planar representation has implications on the original graph (similarly to Lemma 2.5
above, which shows how the effect of contraction in the planar representation can be lifted back to the
original graph). In this situation, this lifting is extremely delicate and nontrivial, especially if the set X
is close to some of the islands. Nevertheless, we overcome these difficulties and manage to construct a
(1 + ε)-approximately equivalent weighted instance that has a tree decomposition where the optimal
solution has only 2poly(1/ε) different possible intersections with each bag. Then the 2poly(1/ε) · nO(1)
running time follows by dynamic-programming techniques.
3
Preliminaries
Graphs, walks, paths, distances
We use standard graph notation [52] unless stated otherwise. The
graph G has vertex set V (G) and edge set E(G). A walk P is an alternating sequence of vertices and
edges, starting and ending with a vertex. A walk is closed if its start- and endpoint are the same vertex,
it is a path if each of its vertices appears exactly once in P, and it is a cycle if it is a closed walk where
all vertices are non-repeating except for the start and end vertex being identical. The length of P is
denoted by |P|, and it is the number of edges in the sequence of P. In particular, we allow for length-0
paths that consist of a single vertex. For a cycle, path, or walk P the shortest path (or walk) from u to
v along P is denoted by P[u, v]. We will also use P(u, v) to refer to the subpath/subwalk P[u, v] with
the first and last vertex removed.
The shortest-path distance of u, v ∈V (G) is denoted by distG(u, v) and is simply the number of
edges on a shortest path with endpoints u and v. For a vertex set X ⊂V (G) let N(X, k) be the set of
vertices within distance k from X, that is, N(X, k) = {v ∈V (G) | ∃x ∈X : distG(x, v) ⩽k}. We
also use the notation N(v, k) when talking about the neighborhood of one vertex, and N(X) (resp.,
N(v)) is a shorthand for N(X, 1) (resp., N(v, 1)), i.e., the closed neighborhood of X (resp., the closed
neighborhood of v).
Partitions, contractions, and consolidations
Let G be a graph and let P be a partition of V (G).
For a vertex v ∈V (G) or vertex set X ⊆V (G), let vP or XP denote the partition class of v or the
set of partition classes in P intersected by X, respectively. Given a vertex set X and a partition Q of
some vertex set Y ⊆V (G), we denote by X Q the set where vertices of X that fall in the same class
of Q are identified. The consolidation has a corresponding consolidation map ψ : X →X Q where
17


## Page 21


ψ(u) = ψ(v) if and only if u = v or u and v are in the same class of Q.
For a graph G and a partition Q of some subset Y of V (G), let GQ denote the consolidation that
one gets by identifying the vertex set C for each C ∈Q, and deleting loops and parallel edges. More
precisely, V (G Q) := V (G) Q, and
E(G Q) = {ψ(C)ψ(C′) | C, C′ ∈Q1 ∧∃uv ∈E(G) : (u ∈C ∧v ∈C′)},
where Q1 is the partition of V (H) that extends Q into a partition of V (G) by adding each vertex of
V (G) \ Y as a singleton, and ψ is the consolidation map for V (G) Q. We will usually work with a
partition P of V (G) itself where each class of P induces a clique in G. We will then abbreviate G P
as GP, and usually think of P as the vertex set of GP.
If F is an edge set in G, then we denote by G/F the contraction of the edges of G, where we
remove loops and parallel edges. If QF denotes the partition of G[F] into its connected components,
then G/F = G QF . For a graph G and a vertex set X ⊂V (G), let G X denote the graph where the
edges induced by X are contracted, i.e., G/E(G[X]). For a vertex set A ⊂G, let V (G X)|A denote
the vertices of G X whose pre-image contains some vertex of A.
Treewidth and P-flattened treewidth.
A tree decomposition of a graph G is a tree graph T whose
nodes are subsets of V (G) called bags, with the following properties:
(i) S
B∈V (T ) B = V (G), i.e., each vertex of G is represented in some bag of T
(ii) For each edge uv ∈E(G) there is some B ∈V (T ) with u, v ∈B, that is, each edge of G is
induced by some bag
(iii) For each v ∈V (G) the bags {B ∈V (T ) | v ∈B} form a connected subtree of T .
The width of the tree decomposition T is maxB∈V (T ) |B| −1, and the treewidth of G is the mini-
mum width among its tree decompositions, and it is denoted by tw(G).
Let ω : V (G) →R be a weight function. The weighted width of a tree decomposition T of G is
maxB∈V (T ) ω(B). The weighted treewidth of (G, ω) is the minimum weighted width among its tree
decompositions.
Suppose that P is a partition of V (G). The P-flattened treewidth [46] of G is the weighted treewidth
of GP under the weight function ω : P →R⩾0 defined as ω(C) := log(|C| + 1) for each C ∈P. We
denote P-flattened treewidth of G by twP(G).
Note that in an n-vertex graph G and any partition P of V (G) we have twP(G) = O(log n) ·
tw(G P).
Observation 3.1. Let T be a candidate tree decomposition of G where all vertices and edges of G are
represented in some bag of T . Then T is a valid tree decomposition of G if and only if for every connected
subgraph H ⊂G the set of bags containing some vertex of H form a connected subtree of T.
Proof. Clearly if any connected subgraph H has this porperty, then in particular one-vertex edgeless
subgraphs also have this property, which directly yields property (iii) in the definition. On the other
hand, if T is a valid tree decomposition, then let us use induction on V (H) to prove that the vertices
of V (H) form a connected subtree. The claim holds for |V (H)| = 1 by condition (iii) of tree decom-
positions. Suppose that v ∈V (H), and let Tv be the subtree of T where v appears in the bag. By
induction, there is also a subtree TH−v that contains the bags where some vertex of H −v appears. Let
u ∈NH(v). By property (ii) of tree decompositions, there is some bag Buv containing both u and v.
Since Buv is a vertex of both Tv and TH−v, we get that the union of these trees is a connected subtree
of H, concluding the proof.
18


## Page 22


Subset TSP and Steiner tree.
We formally define our optimization problems as follows.
Subset TSP
Input: A graph G and a vertex set T ⊆V (G) called terminals.
Output: A shortest closed walk W of G where T ⊂V (W).
Steiner Tree
Input: A graph G and a vertex set T ⊆V (G) called terminals.
Output: A minimum size vertex set S ⊆V (G) where T ⊆S and G[S] is connected.
Notice that our definition of Steiner Tree is non-standard, as one normally has an edge-weighted
graph and one is looking for a subgraph of edge weight k that contains all the terminals. In our
unweighted setting these two optimization problems are equivalent from the perspective of approxi-
mation: indeed, a solution of size |S| = k yields has a spanning tree of size k −1, and a Steiner tree
with k −1 edges has k vertices. Thus (up to changing ε by a constant factor) an approximate solution
for one problem is yields an approximate solution for the other with a linear time overhead.
Objects, regions, and curves
For a closed planar set (object or region) R ⊂R2 we denote by ∂R its
boundary. We denote by Int R and Ext R the interior and exterior of R, respectively. For an arbitrary
planar set X we denote by Cl(X) its closure. A continuous function γ : [0, 1] →R2 is a curve
between its endpoints γ(0) and γ(1). We will often abuse notation and refer to the picture of γ (i.e.,
γ([0, 1])) as γ. A curve γ is piecewise linear if it is the concatenation of closed segments at endpoints
shared between consecutive segments. A curve is a closed curve when γ(0) = γ(1). The curve is
self-intersecting if γ(x) = γ(y) for some x ̸= y where {x, y} ̸= {0, 1}. A non-self-intersecting closed
curve is a Jordan curve.
Polygons and intersection graphs
A simple polygon is a closed bounded region of R2 whose
boundary is a piecewise linear closed Jordan curve. In particular, a segment is not considered to be a
simple polygon. A non-self-intersecting polygon is a connected bounded region of R2 whose bound-
ary consists of pairwise disjoint piecewise linear Jordan curves. If P is a non-self-intersecting polygon,
then the closures of the bounded components of R2 \ P are called the holes of P; since the holes are
bounded by piecewise linear Jordan curves, they are simple polygons. We observe that a component
of the union of some non-self-intersecting polygons where no three polygon vertices are collinear is a
non-self-intersecting polygon.
The complexity of a non-self-intersecting polygon is its number of vertices, and the complexity of an
intersection graph that is represented by non-self-intersecting polygons is the total number of vertices
in the representing polygons. For a polygon v and graph G let compl(v) and compl(G) denote their
complexity, respectively. For a set of polygons the arrangement given by the polygons is the partition
of the plane into a set of faces, edges and vertices given by the drawing of the polygon boundaries
in the plane. The arrangement of an intersection graph G of polygons is simply the arrangement of
its polygons, and it is denoted by A(G). The complexity of an arrangement is the complexity of its
elements, i.e., the total number of vertices, edges and faces, and is denoted by compl(A(G)). Notice that
a set of compl(G) segments can create at most
 compl(G)
2

intersections, so Euler’s formula implies that
compl(A(G)) = O(compl(G)2). We use V (A(G)) to refer to the set of vertices in the arrangement,
i.e., this set includes both the polygon vertices and the intersection points of their edges.
An intersection graph G is graph whose vertices are objects (sets) in some fixed metric space —
typically the plane—, and its edges are the unordered pairs of intersecting objects. Objects are typically
denoted by lowercase letters, and subgraphs in G are thought of as a collection of vertices and edges.
The intersection graphs in this article are given via their representation, that is, by some straightfor-
ward description of the objects V (G): in particular, unit disks are defined via the coordinates of their
disk centers, and non-self-intersecting polygons are defined by listing the coordinates of the polygon
vertices on each boundary component in clockwise order.
19


## Page 23


We will also work with graphs that are represented as plane graphs, i.e., vertices are points (or
sometimes identical to points), and its edges are associated with piecewise linear curves between the
corresponding endpoints. For a graph G with such a representation, we typically use the notation eG.
If the curves representing the edges are piecewise linear, then compl( eG) denotes the total number of
internal vertices on its edge curves plus the number of vertices in G; similarly, if γ is a piecewise linear
curve, then compl(γ) is the number of vertices and edges in γ.
Fair and α-standard intersection graphs.
Definition 3.2 (Fair and α-standard intersection graphs). We say that an intersection graph G
of simple polygons in the plane is fair if for any two polygons v, w ∈V (G) each component of the
intersection v∩w is a simple polygon and no three polygon edges (from the polygons in V (G)) meet at
one point. If additionally each polygon v ∈V (G) contains a closed disk of radius 1 and has diameter
diam(v) ⩽α for some fixed α > 1, then G is called α-standard.
We denote by Iα the class of intersection graphs with an α-standard embedding. Notice that a
polygon of an α-standard graph contains a closed axis-aligned unit square in its interior, since this
already holds for the disk of radius 1 contained in the polygon. Since an axis-aligned unit square
contains some grid point from Z2, we can associate each object with the lexicographically smallest2
point of Z2 contained in the interior of the polygon.
Definition 3.3 (Cell partition). The cell partition or clique partition of a graph G ∈Iα (given by its
representation) is the partition P of V (G) where u, v ∈V (G) are in the same class of P if and only if
the lexicographically smallest grid point contained in the interior of u and v are the same grid point.
In particular, all objects assigned to the same class are stabbed by a single point, thus for any C ∈P
we have that G[C] is a clique. Occasionally, we will refer to the grid point associated with a class C of
P as ˙C, and the set of points associated with the classes of P as ˙P.
In Appendix A we show the following theorem about converting intersection graphs into α-
standard graphs.
Theorem 3.4. Let G be an intersection graph of (a) δ-similarly sized disks or (b) δ-similarly sized β-
fat polygons given by their representation. Then in polynomial time, we can construct an α-standard
intersection graph G′ that is isomorphic to G whose representation complexity is polynomial in n, where
α = 36/(βδ) and β = 1 for disks.
3.1
Object frames and wireframes
A frame can be thought of as a planar graph that relates to some underlying intersection graph but can
use vertices of said graph multiple times. More precisely, we define two types of frames as follows.
Definition 3.5 (Object Frame). An object frame of a fair intersection graph G is an intersection graph
H that is planar together with a function p : V (H) →V (G) that satisfies the following properties.
1. Each vertex v ∈V (H) is a simple polygon, and has a parent polygon p(v) ∈V (G) such that
v ⊆p(v).
2. For any v, w ∈V (H) they are either disjoint or v ∩w is a segment of positive length.
3. The common intersection of any three distinct polygons v1, v2, v3 ∈V (H) is empty.
Definition 3.6 (Wireframe). A wireframe of a fair intersection graph G is a plane graph eH and a
function p : V ( eH) →V (G) that satisfies the following properties.
2The lexicographic order of Z2 is a total ordering where we order first by x-coordinates and then by y-coordinates, that
is, (a, b) < (c, d) if and only if a < c or a = c and b < d.

either p( ˙u) = p(˙v) and γ ˙u ˙v ⊂Int p( ˙u), or p( ˙u) ̸= p(˙v) and for some t ∈(0, 1) we have
