set (consequently, it does not contain any segments) and it contains no vertex of the arrange-
ment A(G).
We note here that when constructing a wireframe eH for some subgraph or object frame H of G,
it is convenient to disregard condition 3 of wireframes during the construction, as one can perturb
the vertices of a wireframe (while staying in the same face of the arrangement S
v∈V (G) ∂v) so that
the edges of the piecewise-linear curves in E( eH) do not align with any of the edges of ∂v (when
v ∈V (G) \ V (H) or pass through any of the vertices of A(G). This insight can be summarized as
follows.
Observation 3.7 (Perturbation principle). Let H be a subgraph or object frame of G, and let eH be a
wireframe of H. Then in O(compl(G)+compl( eH)) time we can perturb the vertices of the edge curves of
eH to get a wireframe eH′ of both H and G that is isomorphic to eH and has the same complexity. Moreover,
if the vertices of eH are disjoint from the vertices of the polygons of V (G), then V ( eH) = V ( eH′), i.e., it is
sufficient to perturb some of the internal vertices of the edge curves of eH.
We can convert between wireframe and object frame representations using the following lemmas.
Lemma 3.8. Let G be an n-vertex fair intersection graph hosting an object frame H, and for each v ∈
V (H) let ˙v ∈v be a given designated point that is not a polygon vertex for any polygon of V (H)∪V (G),
and not on the shared boundary of any pair of objects v, w ∈V (H). Then in polynomial time we can
build a wireframe eH of G on the vertex set {˙v | v ∈V (H)} isomorphic to H where compl( eH) ⩽
n2 · compl(H).
Proof. For each edge vw ∈E(H) we wish to fix a simple polygonal curve γ(˙v ˙w) with at most
compl(v)+compl(w) vertices from ˙v to ˙w that is covered by v∪w and intersects the segment (∂v∩∂w)
in exactly one internal point. The object frame properties ensure that the objects neighboring v will
intersect v in exactly one segment somewhere along ∂v.

Fix some triangulation of v, and consider each segment s = ˙a˙b of this triangulation that is not
on the boundary of v, see Figure 8. We will also assume that ˙v ̸∈s and handle this case near the
end of the proof. Note that s splits v into two polygons; let vs denote the polygon among these that
does not contain ˙v. Let w1, . . . , wks be the sequence of neighbors of v in order of their appearance
on ∂vs \ s when traversing ∂vs \ s from ˙a to ˙b. We subdivide s into ks + 1 equal length segments,
and label the endpoints from ˙a to ˙b with ˙a, ˙sw1, . . . , ˙swks, ˙b. Furthermore, for each w ∈NH(v) we
fix some point ˙xvw that is an internal point of the segment v ∩w. We can now define the first half
of the edge curve of ˙v ˙w from ˙v to ˙xvw as the piecewise linear curve given by the vertex sequence
˙v, ˙sw
1 , ˙sw
2 , . . . , ˙xvw, where s1, s2, . . . are the unique sequence of triangulation edges separating v from
the triangle containing v ∩w. (Note that when v is a triangle, then this curve consists of a single
segment from ˙v directly to ˙xvw) Notice that there are at most compl(v) −3 internal edges in the
triangulation, thus the half-edge described above has at most compl(v) −1 vertices; together with the
other half of the edge in ˙w, this gives at most compl(v) + compl(w) −3 vertices (as ˙xvw appears on
both half-edges).
It remains to show that this results in a plane graph, i.e., that there are no intersections between
edges. Suppose the contrary, there is an intersection point. Notice that no segment of an edge curve
will align with a triangulation edge, and the edge curves use different vertices. We can also notice
that each half-edge-curve consists of segments in distinct triangles, thus no self-intersection occurs on
these curves. Thus an intersection can only happen between two edges connecting distinct boundary
points of some triangle T = ∆(˙a˙b˙c) of the triangulation of v. Let s = ˙a˙b be the edge of T that separates
˙v from the the interior of T. Let w1, w2 be the neighbors of v whose half-edge-curves intersect in T,
and assume without loss of generality that w1 appears before w2 on ∂vs \ s when traversing ∂vs \ s
from ˙a to ˙b. If both v ∩w1 and v ∩w2 are separated from ˙v by s′ := ˙a˙c, then the segments ˙sw1( ˙s′)w1
and ˙sw2( ˙s′)w2 cannot intersect as w1 ∩v and w2 ∩v will appear in the same order on ∂vs′ \ s′ as it
did on ∂vs \ s, so these points appear in the same order on the segments ˙a˙b and ˙a˙c. The analogous
argument works if both v ∩w1 and v ∩w2 are separated from ˙v by s′′ := ˙c˙b. Finally, due to the order
of appearance of v ∩w1 and v ∩w2 on ∂v the only remaining option is that v ∩w1 is separated from ˙v
by s′ = ˙a˙c and v ∩w2 is separated from ˙v by s′′ = ˙c˙b. Again this means that ˙sw1( ˙s′)w1 and ˙sw2( ˙s′′)w2
are disjoint.
Recall that the above proof assumed that ˙v does not fall on any internal edge of the triangulation of
v. If this happens, then we can simply remove the edge of the triangulation containing ˙v and proceed
with the proof unchanged: if there is some w ∈NH(v) that intersects ∂v on one of the triangles
incident to ˙v, then ˙v will be directly connected to ˙xvw inside that triangle, and such half-edge-curves
cannot introduce intersections with other edges starting from ˙v.
The resulting plane graph eH is a wireframe of H. We apply the perturbations of Observation 3.7
to ensure that the result also satisfies condition 3 of wireframes with respect to G. This results in a
wireframe of complexity at most compl( eH) < E(H) · compl(H) < n2 · compl(H).
Lemma 3.9. Let G be an n-vertex fair intersection graph hosting a wireframe eH. Then in polynomial
time we can construct an object frame H isomorphic to eH of complexity compl(H) ⩽n2 · compl( eH).
Proof. Consider cutting the curve of each edge γ(˙v ˙w) of eH at some point ˙x ˙v ˙w such that the portion
of the edge curve from ˙v to ˙x ˙v ˙w is covered by p(˙v) and the rest is covered by p( ˙w); let us call these
parts half-edge curves. The star at ˙v is defined as the union of the half-edge curves incident to ˙v.
Since deg(˙v) ⩽n −1 and ˙v ∈Int p(˙v), we can construct a polygon u ˙v of complexity n · compl( eH)
for ˙v in some small ε-neighborhood of the star of ˙v such that u ˙v ⊂p(˙v) and for each of the points
{ ˙x ˙v ˙w | ˙w ∈N e
H(˙v)} there is a side s ˙v ˙w ⊂p(˙v)∩p( ˙w) of u ˙v which contains ˙xvw as an inner point. It is
straightforward to check that if ε is small enough, then these polygons are either disjoint or intersect
in segments s ˙v ˙w, thus they form an object frame of G that is isomorphic to eH. The resulting object
frame H has complexity at most compl(H) ⩽n2 · compl( eH).
Finally, we will often restrict our intersection graphs to various regions as follows.

Definition 3.10 (R-restriction). Let G be a fair interection graph, and let R be a simple polygon
such that for any v ∈V (G) each component of the intersection v ∩R is a simple polygon. Then the
R-restriction of G is the intersection graph induced by the simple polygons S
v∈V (G) CComp(v ∩R),
where CComp(X) is the set of connected components of X. We denote the R-restriction of G by G|R.
For a vertex v ∈G|R we denote by ˆv the vertex of G such that v ∈CComp(ˆv ∩R).
For a wireframe eH the drawing of eH, denoted by γ( eH), is the union of all curves in the fixed plane
representation of eH.
If R is a region that is bounded by a wireframe cycle eR of G, then there is a natural restriction of
the parent relation p(.) to R: for ˙v ∈eR we define p|R(˙v) to be the unique object v ∈V (G|R) such that
˙v ∈v and ˆv = p(˙v). We say that a wireframe eW of G is a boundaried wireframe of R = G|R if its
representation is in the closed region R and it has no vertices on ∂R except the vertices of eR.
4
Constructing a connectivity-preserving object frame
We start by defining an intersection graph that is a mix between a fair intersection graph and an object
frame. An intersection graph is fair-frame if its objects are simple polygons, and for any pair u, v of
objects, either (i) each component of u ∩v is a simple polygon or (ii) u ∩v is a segment of positive
length.
In order to prove our next theorem, we need the following technical lemma, which can be consid-
ered as a conversion step from a fair graph to an object frame.
Lemma 4.1. Let G be a fair-frame graph with a fixed vertex v, and consider the connected components of
G−v. Let U1, . . . , Uk denote the unions of the objects in these connected components, and assume that k ⩾
2. Then v has a collection v1, . . . , vt of subpolygons for some t ⩽k−1 such that V (G−v)∪{v1, . . . , vt}
induces a connected fair-frame graph where all vi are pairwise disjoint, and for each vi and u ∈V (G)−v
the intersection vi ∩u is either empty or a segment of positive length. Finally, the new objects have total
complexity Pt
i=1 compl(vi) ⩽3compl(v \ (Sk
j=1 Uk)).
Proof. Consider the drawing of the boundaries of ∂v and ∂Ui for all i. These boundaries slice the
plane into several connected regions, see Figure 9. Notice moreover that in the fair-frame graph it is
possible that ∂Ui ⊂∂v; in this case we think of the shared segment as a distinct degenerate region. We
label each region with the set of objects among v, U1, . . . , Uk that cover the region. Thus there are at
most 2k + 2 distinct labels. These regions then define a planar graph H whose vertices correspond to
regions and edges correspond to region pairs whose shared boundary contains a segment of positive
length. Since v is connected, we have that the planar graph H restricted to the regions inside v is
also connected; let Hv denote this graph. Notice that since each Ui intersects v, the graph Hv must
contain a vertex of label {v, Ui} for each Ui. Next, for each Ui, we identify the set of vertices in Hv
that have the label {v, Ui} into a single vertex, and denote by ui the newly created vertex. We remove
any multiple edges created by the operation. (We note that loops are not created by the identification
as regions of equal label are not adjacent.) Consider some spanning tree of the resulting graph, and
remove all leaves of label {v}, resulting in the tree T.
We claim that T has at most k −1 vertices that are labeled with {v}. Indeed, T has at most k
leaves (namely, each vertex ui may be a leaf), and a vertex of label {v} is adjacent only to new vertices,
while new vertices are only adjacent to vertices of label {v}, as the regions Ui are pairwise disjoint.
Consequently, T is bipartite where one part consists of the vertices of label {v}, and each of these
vertices have degree at least two, while the other part consists of the k vertices ui for i = 1, . . . , k.
This means that if there are t vertices with label {v}, then the edge count is t + k −1 ⩾2t, which
implies t ⩽k −1, as claimed.
Let w be a vertex of T with label {v}, and for each adjacent vertex uj we fix a positive length
segment sj that is on the intersection of the region of w and (one of) the region(s) corresponding to uj.
(More precisely, we choose sj to be a segment inside v that is part of the boundary of a single object

Theorem 4.2. Let G be a fair intersection graph, and let X ⊂V (G) be a set of terminals. Moreover,
each terminal x ∈X has a designated point ˙x ∈x in the plane such that the designated points of distinct
terminals do not coincide. Then there exists an object frame G′ of G where for each x ∈X there is a
corresponding terminal vertex x′ in V (G′) (where p(x′) = x), and the following hold:
1. For each x ∈X we have ˙x ∈x′.
2. Terminals u, v ∈X are in the same connected component of G if and only if u′, v′ are in the same
connected component of G′.
3. |V (G′)| −|V (G)| ⩽16|X|.
4. The object frame G′ is a forest.
5. compl(G′) = O(compl(A(G))).
Finally, when |X| ⩽2, then |V (G′)| ⩽|V (G)|.
Proof. If |X| = 1, then the object frame consisting of the single terminal x ∈X satisfies all the
conditions. For the rest of the proof, assume |X| ⩾2.
We will define a procedure that creates the desired graph G′ using several modifications to G;
for convenience, we refer to the current graph as G, and assume without loss of generality that G is a
connected fair-frame graph, as we can do the following procedure on each of its connected components.
(In turn, we will show that the resulting object frame G′ is a tree.)
It will be convenient to assume in the proof that X consists of pairwise disjoint objects. This can be
achieved the following way. If |X| = 2 and the two objects x, y ∈X intersect, then the object frame
satisfying the statement of the theorem can be found the following way. If ˙x ∈y, then we set x′ to be
24


## Page 28


a triangle in a small neighborhood of ˙x (so that the triangle is in x and it does not contain ˙y), and we
set y′ to be a subpolygon of y that contains ˙y and shares some boundary segment with this triangle.
Otherwise, let y′ = y and let x′ be a subpolygon of x that contains ˙x, internally disjoint from y, and
shares a boundary segment with y. (This is possible as x ∩y cannot be a single point in a fair-frame
graph.) In both cases, we get the required object frame.
If |X| ≥3 and some terminals intersect, then we modify X the following way. Let us place a
new infinitesimally small polygon x′ in each polygon x, so that the created polygons are pairwise
disjoint, and x′ covers ˙x. We redefine X to be the set of these new polygons, and consider them the
new terminals; note that this increases the vertex count of G by |X| and retains the fairness of the
representation, as well as the connectivity between terminals.
We define the rank of a vertex v ∈V (G) \ X (during any time in this procedure) as the number of
connected components of G −v that contain some vertex from X; we denote this number by rank(v).
Furthermore, we fix some root vertex r ∈X. We then apply the following modifications exhaustively:
M1 If for some v ∈V (G) \ X the graph G −v has a component that does not contain any vertex of
X, then delete all vertices in this component.
M2 If rank(v) = 1 for some v ∈V (G) \ X, then we remove v.
M3 If rank(v) ⩾2 for some v ∈V (G) \ X and some vertex w ∈V (G) −v intersects Int v, then
let U1, . . . , Uk be the components of G −v, where U1 is the component containing the root r.
By Lemma 4.1, there exists a collection of at most k −1 subpolygons v′

M4 For any point p ∈R2 that is on the boundary of least three polygons v ∈V (G), we remove a
small triangle or concave quadrilateral around p from all but one polygon that contains it; note
that this does not change the realized intersection graph.
After exhausting modifications M1, M2, and M3, the realizing polygons are interior-disjoint. After
M4, the resulting intersection graph is an object frame of the original graph. Moreover, each polygon
in V (G) \ X is a cut vertex, as otherwise it could be removed by M2.
We now continue the procedure with the following modifications.

u ∩v contained a terminal point ˙x, then ˙x is still covered by u.)

the number of sides of any given polygon, and M5 can be executed without increasing the complexity
of the polygons in question. As a result, we have that compl(G′) ⩽compl(G) + 2 · 3compl(A(G)) =
O(compl(A(G)).
Recall that after applying M5 exhaustively we get a tree. Each leaf of this tree must be a terminal
from X. Thus, the tree has at most |X| −2 vertices of degree at least 3, and there are at most 2|X| −2
edge-disjoint paths whose internal vertices have degree 2 and whose endpoints are leaves or branching
in the tree. Consequently, there are at most 8|X| −8 + 2|X| −2 < 10|X| vertices that are within
distance 2 from a branching point. Furthermore, if x ∈X is not a branching point, then there are at
most 5 vertices that are within distance 2 from x and not within distance two of a branching, so in
total there are at most 15|X| vertices in G′ that are within distance 2 of a vertex of X or a branching.
Thus, after applying M6 exhaustively, each of the n original objects has at most one subpolygon that
is at distance at least 3 from all branching vertices and all vertices from X. Consequently, there are
at most n + |X| + 15|X| polygons. Thus the resulting object frame is a tree with at most n + 16|X|
vertices, as required.
Finally, notice that when |X| = 2, then either the terminals intersect and |G′| = 2, or the the two
terminals are disjoint. In this latter case, we did not introduce any new terminals, and by definition the
rank of any object is at most 2. In particular, M2 can replace a vertex v of rank k = 2 with k −1 = 1
new object, so the number of objects is not increased by the modifications.
If we apply the above theorem on a shortest path (with the endpoints being the terminals) then we
get an isomorphic representation.
Corollary 4.3. Let P be a shortest path from u to v in G, where ˙u ∈u and ˙v ∈v. Then there exists a
wireframe eP in G of complexity compl( eP) = O(compl(A(P))) inducing a path from ˙u to ˙v where the
parent relation p(.) gives an isomorphism to P.
Proof. We consider the intersection graph given by the objects along P and set X = {u, v} with
designated points ˙u and ˙v. Then Theorem 4.2 gives an object frame of complexity O(A(P)) where
|V (G′)| ⩽|V (GP )| where the end objects are connected with an object frame. Since all edges of the
object frame must correspond to edges of P, the only way for this connection to occur is if there is a
bijection between E(P) and E(G′), which in turn implies that the graphs are isomorphic under the
parent relation. This isomorphism is preserved when we switch to a wireframe representation using
Lemma 3.8. Finally, we apply Observation 3.7 to make this wireframe of P into a wireframe of G
without increasing its complexity.
We will use the above corollary several times to define wireframe paths, but we will usually need
a finer control over the complexity of the resulting paths. This is provided by the following lemma for
a more specific setting. Essentially, we want to say that when we move from a region R to a smaller

Lipschitz embeddings into plane graphs
Let G, H be graphs. We say that a mapping f : V (G) →V (H) is c-Lipschitz for some constant c ⩾1
if for any pair of vertices x, y ∈V (G) we have that
1
c · distG(x, y) ⩽distH(f(x), f(y)) ⩽c · distG(x, y).
(1)
Clearly any c-Lipschitz mapping is injective.
Observation 5.1. The natural mapping f from G ∈Iα to GP satisfies
1
3distG(x, y) ⩽distGP(f(x), f(y)) ⩽distG(x, y)
for all x, y with f(x) ̸= f(y).
Proof. The upper bound of distGP(f(x), f(y)) ⩽distG(x, y) follows from the fact that GP is obtained
from G by edge contractions, which cannot increase distances.
For any edge f(u)f(v) ∈E(GP) there is a path of length at most 3 connecting u and v in G, and
therefore distGP(f(u), f(v)) ⩽3distG(u, v) holds. The same inequality follows for any u, v where
f(u) ̸= f(v) by applying the above inequality on all edges of the shortest f(u)−f(v) path in GP.
The goal of this section is to show that GP can be Lipschitz-mapped to a planar graph on the same
vertex set as GP, and then to use this to show that G itself can be Lipschitz-mapped to a planar graph
with the same vertex set as G. For a clique C ∈P, let ˙C denote the stabbing point of C, and let ˙P
be the set of centers of non-empty cells. Recall that we can also think of GP as an intersection graph,
where the object corresponding to a clique C ∈P is the union of the objects in C. The core theorem
of this section is the following.
28


## Page 32


Theorem 5.2. For any α > 0, there exists a positive constant c = O(α32) such that for any G ∈Iα
given by its representation the following holds. Let P be the cell partition of G. Then in polynomial time
one can construct a plane graph H = ( ˙P, E′) such that natural mapping from P to ˙P is c-Lipschitz from
GP to H. Moreover, H has maximum degree O(α34), its edges have geometric diameter at most O(α17)
and each edge of H can be covered by at most O(α34) objects of GP.
Before we begin the proof, note that the theorem implies the analogous statement for G itself.
Corollary 5.3. For any α > 0 there exists a positive constant c = O(α32) such that for any G ∈Iα
given by its representation, one can in polynomial time construct a plane graph G′ = (V (G), E′) such
that the identity mapping of V (G) is c-Lipschitz from G to G′.
Proof. Let H be the plane graph on ˙P created by Theorem 5.2, and to each vertex ˙C of H we attach
|C| −1 leaves, and assign the original vertex and the leaves arbitrarily to distinct vertices of C. It is
routine to check that with the resulting planar graph G′ the above map is 3c-Lipschitz, where c is the
Lipschitz-constant guaranteed by Theorem 5.2.
The following lemma will be used much later to help us get rid of unwanted vertices using a con-
traction, while maintaining a Lipschitz map.
Lemma 5.4. Let G be a graph with vertex set V , and let H be a graph with vertex set W such that
V ⊆W. Suppose moreover that the identity map f : V →V is c-Lipschitz from G to H. Then in
polynomial time (in terms of |W|) one can construct a set of edges S in H such that the vertices of the
contraction H/S correspond to the vertices of V and this map is c2-Lipschitz from G to H/S.
Moreover, if H is a plane graph whose edges are piece-wise linear curves of geometric diameter at
most δ, then for any µ > 0 the graph H/S can be realized as a plane graph whose edges have geometric
diameter at most δ · (c + 2), and each edge of H/S is contained in the geometric µ-neighborhood of some
path of H.
Proof. Let us show first that we can assume that each vertex w ∈W has distance at most c/2 from
some vertex in V . We claim that the identity map remains c-Lipschitz from G to H if we remove every
vertex of H that is at distance more than c/2 from every vertex in V . Indeed, each edge of G can be
realized with a path of length at most c in H connecting two vertices of V , and no vertex on such a
path was removed. Consequently, any shortest path of length k in G can be covered by the union of at
most k such paths of length c. On the other hand, deleting vertices from H can only increase pairwise
distances, so the inequality 1
cdistG(u, v) ⩽distH(u, v) remains true after the deletion. Therefore, in
the following we can assume that each vertex w ∈W has distance at most c/2 from some vertex in V .
Let V = {v1, . . . , vn}, and define the following vertex sets in H. The Voronoi cell Vor(vi) of a

of V (breaking ties by the order of i). More precisely, we define

concludes the proof that H[Vor(vi)] is connected.

5.1
Approximate plane graph with extra vertices
In order to prove Theorem 5.2, we need to start with an intermediate step, which resembles known
contraction decomposition theorems for unit disks [10,74]: initially, we also allow additional vertices
in the plane graph, and later we will remove the extra vertices so that only the clique centers ˙P remain.
Let us fix a graph G ∈Iα and its representation, as well as a cell-partition P based on Z2. Each
edge CC′ of GP can be associated with some edge uu′ of G where u ∈C and u′ ∈C′. If eP is a
single-edge wireframe of G with endpoints ˙C and ˙C′, where pγ( ˙C) ∈C and pγ( ˙C′) ∈C′, then we
say that the curve γ = γ( eP) is an edge curve of G with respect to the partition P, which corresponds
to the edge CC′ of GP. An edge curve can be constructed by applying Corollary 4.3 to the single-edge
shortest path uu′ of G and the fixed endpoints ˙C ∈Int u and ˙C′ ∈Int u′. We construct for each
edge of GP a corresponding edge curve, and denote by Γ the resulting set of edge curves. We denote
by I(Γ) the intersection graph induced by Γ. Observe that the curves are drawn in an open region,
thus —similarly to Observation 3.7— they can be defined in such way to satisfy the following general

position requirements: (i) the curves of Γ avoid ˙P, that is, no curve passes through any point a ∈˙P
other than at its endpoints, (ii) distinct edge curves intersect each other in O(A(G)) points, and (iii)
no three edge curves meet at any point of R2 \ ˙P.
Since each object of G has diameter at most α, and an edge curve can be covered by the union of
two objects that intersect, we have the following observations.
Observation 5.5. The edge curves of Γ satisfy the following properties.
(i) Each point ˙C is incident to at most O(α2) edge curves.
(ii) Each edge curve γ ∈Γ has geometric diameter at most 2α.
(iii) Each edge curve intersects at most O(α4) other edge curves.
We can now prove the following.

construct a plane graph H with ˙P ⊆V (H) such that the natural mapping from P to ˙P is c-Lipschitz
from GP to H for some c = O(α16). Moreover, (i) H has maximum degree at most O(α2), (ii) for each
clique C ∈P the union S

of an edge curve of G and thus each edge of H has geometric diameter at most 2α.

We are now ready to finish the proof of Theorem 5.2.
Proof of Theorem 5.2. Let H0 be the planar graph with extra vertices given by Theorem 5.6. We set µ
small enough so that for any edge curve γ of H covered by a minimal collection T of objects it holds
that the geometric µ-neighborhood of γ is also covered by T. We apply Lemma 5.4 to GP, H0 and
µ. This yields an edge set S and the plane graph H0/S so that H0/S and GP are O(α16)-Lipschitz,
each edge of H0/S is covered by objects from GP, and each edge of H0/S has at most O(α16) times
the geometric diameter of the maximum edge diameter of H0, which —as an edge curve section— had
geometric diameter at most O(α). Thus H := H0/S is a plane graph that can be created in polynomial
time and has the desired Lipschitz property with the Lipschitz-constant
 O(α16)
2 = O(α32), its edges
have geometric diameter at most O(α17) and they are covered by objects of GP. Since there are O(α34)
objects in GP within distance O(α17) of any given point in the plane, we have that O(α34) objects of
GP are sufficient to cover any edge of H.
To prove the degree bound on H, recall that V (H) = ˙P is a subset of the integer grid, so we have
that the number of points within distance O(α17) from a fixed vertex ˙C ∈V (H) is at most O(α34),
thus the maximum degree of H is at most O(α34), which concludes the proof.

eG is at most O(1) + maxC∈P |C| ⩽O(1) + ∆(G). Theorem 5.6 created a graph where the objects in
any clique of G contain O(1) vertices of H in total, and H has degree O(1). We have subdivided each
edge of H at most twice, thus there are O(1) vertices of eG intersected by any given clique (and thus
by any given object) of G. Since an object v ∈V (G) can only be assigned as parent to vertices of eG
that fall inside v, we have that |p−1(v)| = O(1).

distG(x, y) ⩽dist eG( ˙x, ˙y).

Contraction decompositions for Lipschitz-mapped graphs
In order to create our algorithms, we will need to use Baker’s technique [9] to be able to work on a
bounded-treewidth graph. Our starting point is the following contraction decomposition from [51].
Theorem 2.2 (Contraction Decomposition for Planar Graphs, Demaine et al. [51]). For any
integer k ⩾2, the edge set of any planar graph G can be partitioned into k sets E1, . . . , Ek such that
tw(G/Ei) = O(k) for all i ∈[k]. The partition can be found in O(n3/2 log n) time.
Based on the above theorem and our machinery for planarizing intersection graphs via Lipschitz
embeddings, we get a contraction decompostion for intersection graphs. However, we need the follow-
ing key lemma first, which allows us to compare the treewidth of a contraction of a Lipschitz-mapping
of H of G to the treewidth of some (larger) contraction of G. Recall that for a graph G and a vertex set
X ⊂V (G), let G X denote the graph where the edges induced by X are contracted, i.e., G/E(G[X]).
Lemma 2.5. Let G and H be graphs with the same vertex set V , so that the identity map of V is c-
Lipschitz from G to H. Let X ⊂V be arbitrary, and let Xr be the set of vertices whose endpoints have
H-distance at most r from some vertex in X, i.e., we set Xr = NH(X, r). Assume moreover that H has
degree at most ∆. Then tw(G Xc2) = O(∆c) · tw(H X).
Proof. Let ˘X denote the set of contracted vertices in H X, that is, V (H X) is the disjoint union of
V \X and ˘X. Let ˘Xc2 denote the set of vertices contracted in G Xc2, that is, V (G Xc2) is the disjoint
union of V \ Xc2 and ˘Xc2.
We claim that if a, b ∈V are contracted to the same vertex ˘x ∈˘X, then they are also contracted
to the same vertex ˘xc2 ∈˘Xc2. Indeed, since a and b are both contracted to ˘x in H, there exists a
path from a to b in H[X]. By the Lipschitz property, each edge uv of this path can be represented by
a path Puv of length at most c in G. The vertices of Puv are in NG({u, v}, c/2), so by the Lipschitz
35

property, they are in NH({u, v}, c2/2) ⊂Xc2 and thus the concatenation of the paths Puv forms a
connected subgraph of H[Xc2] that contains both u and v. This concludes the proof of the claim. For
a given ˘x ∈˘X, let ϕ(˘x) be the corresponding contracted vertex in ˘Xc2. The previous claim implies
that ϕ : ˘X →˘Xc2 is well-defined and surjective.
Let T be a tree decomposition of H X of width w. Using this decomposition we now create a
tree decomposition T ′ of G Xc2 with the same underlying tree. In what follows, BH denotes a bag
of the tree decomposition of H X, and the corresponding bag in the new decomposition for G Xc2
is denoted by BG. We will furthermore let ψG : V (G) →V (G Xc2) and ψH : V (H) →V (H X)
denote the consolidation maps of the contractions.
For each vertex v ∈BH ∩(V \ X), place all vertices of ψG(NH(v, c)) in BG. For each vertex
v ∈˘X ∩BH we place ϕ(v) in BG. Thus the resulting bags BG have increased their size by a factor of

O(∆c+1)·w. Thus, it remains to show that the resulting decomposition T ′ is a valid tree decomposition
of G Xc2.
Observe that all vertices of G Xc2 are included in some bag by the surjectivity of ϕ. Let uv be

the bag BH contained u′.
Finally, we need to show that the set of bags that contain a given vertex v ∈V (G Xc2) form a
connected subtree of T ′. Suppose first that v ∈V \ Xc2. Then v appears in a bag BG if and only if the
corresponding bag BH contains some vertex of NH(v, c) \ X. Note that since v ̸∈Xc2, we have that
NH(v, c) is disjoint from X, so the condition simplifies to BH containing some vertex of NH(v, c).
By Observation 3.1, the vertices of ψH(NH(v, c)) appear in a connected subtree of T as they induce a
connected subgraph of H X, thus the same holds for the set of bags where v appears in.
Suppose now that v ∈˘Xc2. Let Y be the set of vertices that are contracted to v by the contrac-
tion G Xc2. Clearly G[Y ] is connected, so by the Lipschitz property, we have that the connected
components of H[Y ] can be connected by length c paths, hence, H[NH(Y, c)] is connected. By Ob-
servation 3.1 we have that the vertices ψH(NH(Y, c)) appear in a connected subtree of T as they
induce a connected graph. Notice however that v is added to a bag BG if and only if some vertex of
ψH(NH(Y, c)) appears in the corresponding bag BH. Consequently, the set of vertices containing v
also form a connected subtree in T ′.
We are now ready to state our contraction decomposition theorem.
Theorem 6.1 (Contraction Decomposition for Intersection Graphs). Let G be an intersection
graph of similarly sized fat objects with cell partition P where each clique C ∈P has size at most

cliques of P \ Xi, and the contracted vertices are added as singletons.

Remark 6.2. The bounds (4) and (5) are the only reason why our running time is doubly (or triply,
in case of the slower Steiner tree algorithm) exponential as a function of α. To get single-exponential
dependence on α, one can observe that the size of a c-neighborhood in H is only quadratic in c, as
all the graphs H where Lemma 2.5 is applied as well as the graph H in Theorem 6.1 are not only of
degree at most c, but have further porperties: their vertices are grid points and its edges have length
bounded by some polynomial of α. Thus the size of the c-neighborhood of a vertex of H is poly(α)c2.
7
Sparsifying cliques
The goal of this section is to simplify the underlying graph so that each clique has f(1/ε) vertices,
which will be required for our algorithms. The simplification is done in several steps, and uses some
problem-specific arguments.
7.1
Sparsifying cliques for Subset TSP
Lemma 7.1. Let G be an intersection graph with cell partition P and let T ⊂V (G) be a set of terminals.
Then there is an optimum subset TSP walk W for T that uses O(1) non-terminal vertices from each cell
C ∈P, i.e., |(V (W) \ T) ∩C| = O(1) for each C ∈P. Moreover, all vertices are used O(1) times by
the walk, and all but O(1) vertices of T ∩C ∩V (W) are incident only to edges induced by C.
Proof. We start by simplifying the tour so that no pair of neighboring cells5 has more than two edges
of W going between them. Let C, C′ be two cells such that there are at least three edges of W going
between them. We pick an orientation on W to make it a directed closed walk. As a result, there are at
least two arcs of W going in the same direction between C and C′; suppose without loss of generality
that uu′ and vv′ are edges of W that both go from C to C′. We can then replace the edges uu′ and vv′
in W with the edges uv and u′v′ (and reverse the direction of the u′ to v subpath of W) to get a closed
walk W ′ of the same length that has fewer inter-cell steps. Repeated applications of this step result in
a tour W that has at most two edges going between any pair of neighboring cells.
If a vertex x ∈C \ T has all incident edges ending in C, then x could be bypassed and we would
get a shorter walk spanning all the terminals; therefore, each vertex of C \ T must be incident to at
least one edge that goes to a different cell. Since there are only O(1) cells that a given vertex can be
connected to, and at most two edges in W lead to each of these cells, there are only O(1) non-terminals
in C that are on W. The last claim also follows.
5The cells C and C′ are neighboring if they are neighbors in GP, that is, they may not be “grid-neighbors”.
37


## Page 41


Next, we remove some vertices from the terminal set T (but not from the graph) so that in the new
instance each cell has only a few terminals from the new terminal set.
Lemma 7.2. Given ε > 0 and an instance (G, T) of Subset TSP, we can construct a vertex set X ⊂T
to be removed from the set of terminals in poly(n) time such that the following hold for X and the new
terminal set T ′ = T \ X.
(i) Each C ∈P contains only a few terminals: |C ∩T ′| ⩽1/ε
(ii) If (G, T) has a subset TSP of length ℓthen (G, T ′) has a subset TSP of length
 1 + O(ε)

ℓ−|X|.
(iii) Given a subset TSP of (G, T ′) of length ℓ′, a subset TSP of (G, T) of length ℓ′+|X| can be constructed
in O(n2) time.
Proof. Let P be a cell partition of G. In each clique C, we select up to 1/ε arbitrary terminals per
clique to be put into T ′; in cliques with at most 1/ε terminals, all terminals will be put into T ′. We let
X = T \ T ′ bet he set of terminals to be deleted. Clearly this satisfies (i).
To prove (ii), let W be a subset TSP walk of (G, T) of length ℓthat is simplified with respect to P
in the sense of Lemma 7.1. Consequently, there are O(1) edges of W leaving each clique C ∈P; let
us call such edges external, and let Wext denote their multiset. Edges of W whose endpoints are in the
same clique of P are called internal, and their multiset is denoted by Wint. Note that if a vertex of W is
not incident to any external edges, then it must be a terminal and it is visited only once in W. Indeed,
a non-terminal that is incident to only internal edges can be removed from W, and multiple visitations
of a terminal with internal edges can also be simplified to one visitation. Since we have O(1) external
edges incident to any clique C ∈P, we have that there are at most |C ∩T| internal edges incident
to C.
We say that C is light if |C∩T| ⩽1/ε, and otherwise we say that C is heavy. We can now construct
a subset TSP walk W ′ for the instance (G, T ′) as follows: we follow the walk W, and if in some heavy
clique C we have that both the arc entering and exiting v ∈X ∩V (W)∩C is internal, then we skip v.
Note that the skipping is possible since the predecessor and successor of v are connected in C.

Lemma 7.3. Let G be an intersection graph with cell partition P and terminal set T such that |C∩T| ⩽k
for each C ∈P. Then for any ε > 0 there is a vertex set V ′ ⊆V such that for any C ∈P we have
|V ′ ∩C| = O(k2/ε4), and for any u, v ∈T we have distG[V ′](u, v) ⩽(1 + ε)distG(v, v′). Moreover,
given G and its cell partition P, the set V ′ can be computed in polynomial time.
38


## Page 42


Proof. From each C ∈P choose a single vertex aC to be the hub of C, and collect the hubs in the
vertex set A. Then for any pair of vertices u, v ∈T ∪A of distance at most 4/ε + 2 we add to V ′ all
vertices of a shortest u →v path.
If a vertex w ∈C is added to A, then it is either a hub, a terminal, or it is an internal vertex of a
shortest path connecting some u, v ∈T ∪A with distG(u, v) ⩽4/ε + 2. Consequently, distG(w, u)
and distG(w, v) are both less than 4/ε + 2. The number of vertices in T ∪A at distance at most
4/ε + 2 from w is at most O(k/ε2), therefore the number of such shortest paths is O(k2/ε4), and thus
|V ′ ∩C| = O(k2/ε4).
To show the second property, let u, v ∈T be vertices whose distance is ℓ⩾4/ε. Let u =
u0, u1, . . . , uk = v be a sequence of vertices on a shortest u →v path such that k = ⌈ℓε/4⌉
and distG(ui, ui+1) ⩽4/ε.
Let ai be the hub of the cell Ci ∈P which contains ui.
Since
distG(ui, ui+1) ⩽4/ε, we have that distG(ai, ai+1) ⩽4/ε + 2, therefore V ′ contains a shortest
path from ai, ai+1. Concatenating these paths we get a u →v path whose internal vertices are in V ′
and has length at most ℓ+ 2k = ℓ+ 2⌈ℓε/4⌉< ℓ+ 2 · (ℓε/2) = (1 + ε)ℓ.
Finally, notice that the above construction can be repeated using a polynomial algorithm.
Lemma 7.4. Given ε > 0 and an instance (G, T) of Subset TSP with a fixed cell partition P of G, in
polynomial time we can construct an instance (G′, T ′) that satisfies the following conditions.
• V (G′) ⊆V (G), T ′ ⊆T
• Each clique C ∈P satisfies |C ∩V (G′)| ⩽O(1/ε6) and |C ∩T ′| ⩽1/ε.
• The shortest subset TSP tour of (G′, T ′) is within a 1+O(ε) factor from the optimum of (G, T), and
given a (1 + O(ε))-approximate tour for (G′, T ′) we can in polynomial time create a (1 + O(ε))-
approximate tour for (G, T).
Proof. Fix a cell partition P of G. By Lemma 7.2 we can create a terminal set T ′ where for each C ∈P
the number of terminals is at most |C ∩T ′| ⩽1/ε, and the length of the optimum tour increases by
at most a 1 + O(ε) factor. We can then apply Lemma 7.3 on (G, T ′) with k = 1/ε to get a graph
G′ where each clique C′ has size at most |C′| ⩽1/ε6. Note that any subset TSP walk W can be
regarded as a sequence of shortest paths between its consecutive terminal visits, so by Lemma 7.3 the
shortest subset TSP tour of (G′, T ′) is within a 1 + O(ε) factor from the optimum of (G, T), and given
a (1+O(ε))-approximate tour for (G′, T ′) we can in polynomial time create a (1+O(ε))-approximate
tour for (G, T).
7.2
Sparsifying cliques for Steiner Tree
The next two lemmas are similar to those that we have seen for Subset TSP.
Lemma 7.5. Let G be an intersection graph with cell partition P and let T ⊆V (G) be a set of terminals.
Then given any Steiner tree H0 for (G, T) we can construct in polynomial time a Steiner tree H for
(G, T) where V (H) ⊂V (H0), and H uses O(1) non-terminal vertices from each cell C ∈P, i.e.,
|(V (H) \ T) ∩C| = O(1) for each C ∈P. Moreover, there are at most O(1) vertices of T ∩C ∩V (H)
that are incident to some edge of H that has an endpoint outside C.
Proof. We start by simplifying H0 into a tree H1 so that no pair of neighboring cells has more than
one edge of H1 going between them. Let C, C′ be two cells such that there are at least two edges of
H0 going between them, let (u, u′) and (v, v′) be these edges (where u, v and u′, v′ are not necessarily
distinct). We remove the edge (u, u′) from H0, and as a result the tree falls apart into two components,
one of which contains the edge (v, v′). Without loss of generality, u and v are in different components.
Therefore we can add the edge (u, v) to form a tree of the same length, but which has fewer inter-clique
edges.
39


## Page 43


By applying the above modification exhaustively, we get a a tree H1 where any pair of neighboring
cells have at most one edge going between them. If a vertex x ∈C \ T has all its incident edges in
H1 ending in C, then we bypass x by adding a path connecting its neighbors, and remove x from H1.
Once no more such modifications are possible, the resulting tree H has the property that each vertex
of H in C \ T must be incident to at least one edge that goes to a different cell. Since there are only
O(1) cells that a given vertex can be connected to, and at most one edge in H lead to each of these
cells, there are only O(1) non-terminals in C that are on H. Thus H has the desired properties and it
was obtained in polynomial time.
Lemma 7.6. Given ε > 0 and an instance (G, T) of Steiner Tree, we can construct a vertex set X ⊂T
to be removed from the set of terminals in poly(n) time such that the following hold for X and the new
terminal set T ′ = T \ X.
(i) Each C ∈P contains only a few terminals: |C ∩T ′| ⩽1/ε
(ii) If (G, T) has a Steiner tree of length ℓthen (G, T ′) has a Steiner tree of length
 1 + O(ε)

ℓ−|X|.
(iii) Given a Steiner tree of (G, T ′) of length ℓ′, a Steiner tree of (G, T) of length ℓ′ + |X| can be
constructed in O(n2) time.
Proof. Let P be a cell partition of G. In each clique C, we select up to 1/ε arbitrary terminals per
clique to be put into T ′; in cliques with at most 1/ε terminals, all terminals will be put into T ′. We let
X = T \ T ′ be the set of terminals to be deleted. Clearly this satisfies (i).
To prove (ii), let H be an optimum Steiner tree of (G, T) that is simplified with respect to P in the
sense of Lemma 7.5. As in Lemma 7.2, edges of H leaving a clique C ∈P are external and denoted
by Hext, and the rest of the edges are Hint. Since there are at most O(1) external edges incident to C,
and each non-terminal in C must be incident to at least one such edge, we have that there are at most
|C ∩T| + O(1) internal edges incident to C.
We can now construct a Steiner tree H′ for the instance (G, T ′). To construct the internal edges of
H′, let C be some clique. We say that C is light if |C ∩T| ⩽1/ε, and otherwise we say that C is heavy.
To construct a shorter tree, in each heavy clique C we remove from H any v ∈X ∩C that is only
incident to internal edges. (We can connect the neighbors of v inside the clique with a star after the
removal.) Since there are only O(1) external edges incident to C, this removes at least |X ∩C|−O(1)
vertices. Thus

To prove (iii), let H′ be an optimum tree for the instance (G, T ′). We can then construct the desired

Lemma 7.7. Let G be an intersection graph with cell partition P and terminal set T such that |C∩T| ⩽k

The set V ′ can be computed in 2O((1/ε) log(k/ε))poly(n) time.

computation, the running time is 2O((1/ε) log(k/ε))poly(n), as required.

Lemma 7.8. Given ε > 0 and an instance (G, T) of Steiner Tree with a fixed cell partition P of G, in
2O((1/ε) log(1/ε))poly(n) time we can construct an instance (G′, T ′) that satisfies the following conditions.
• V (G′) ⊆V (G), T ′ ⊆T
• Each clique C ∈P satisfies |C ∩V (G′)| ⩽2O((1/ε) log(1/ε)) and |C ∩T ′| ⩽1/ε.
• The shortest Steiner tree of (G′, T ′) is within a 1 + O(ε) factor from the optimum of (G, T), and
given a (1 + O(ε))-approximate tour for (G′, T ′) we can in polynomial time create a (1 + O(ε))-
approximate tour for (G, T).

A subset spanner for intersection graphs
The main goal of this section is to prove the following theorem.
Theorem 8.1 (Subset spanner). Given G ∈Iα with cell partition P, a vertex set T ⊂V (G) of
terminals, and some ε ∈(0, 1/2), there exists a family S ⊆P of size |S| = O( 1
ε4 StTree(G, T)) such
that for any pair of vertices x, y ∈T we have distG[S S](x, y) ⩽(1 + ε)distG(x, y), and S can be
computed in polynomial time.
41


## Page 45


The first two steps of our proof mirror the subset spanner proof in weighted planar graphs given
by Klein [68]. In Klein’s construction, the spanner is created in three phases: first, a single source
spanner is created, which only concerns distances from a single vertex v to the vertices of a shortest
path Q not containing v. Next, a bipartite spanner is defined that tracks distances from a shortest
uv path Q2 to a different uv path Q1 disjoint from Q2. Finally, a spanner is made that approximates
distances between any pair of vertices on the outer face of a planar graph. It turns out that the first
two steps work in arbitrary, not necessarily planar graphs as well. More precisely Klein’s proof of the
first step (Lemma 8.2 below) does use planarity, but it can be avoided, while the proof of the second
step (Lemma 2.1) can be used verbatim for nonplanar graphs.
Lemma 8.2 (Single source spanner). Let G be a graph, and let Q be a shortest path in G between its
endpoints. Then for any ε > 0 there is a subgraph H with |H| = O(1/ε2)|distG(v, Q)| such that for any
x ∈V (Q) we have distH∪Q(v, x) ⩽(1 + ε)distG(v, x). Moreover, H can be computed in polynomial
time.
Proof. Let R be a shortest path from a vertex v to Q. Let x0 be the endpoint of R on Q, and first we
consider the portion of Q that follows x0. Let Q′ be the portion of Q whose vertices are at distance
at most (1/ε)|R| distance from x0. Starting at x0 we define a sequence of vertices x1, . . . on Q′ the
following way. For i ⩾1, let xi be the earliest vertex on Q′ where distG(v, xi−1) + distG(xi−1, xi) >
(1+ε)distG(v, xi). Analogously, we do the same procedure moving backwards on Q′ from x0, defining
the points x−1, x−2, . . . . Let H be the union of the shortest paths v →xi.
Let xk be the vertex of largest index assigned by the procedure. We estimate the total length of paths
from v to x1, . . . , xk; the paths whose endpoint has a negative index can be estimated analogously. By
summing the inequalities distG(v, xi−1) + distG(xi−1, xi) > (1 + ε)distG(v, xi) defining the xi’s, we
get

Klein [68] shows that applying the single source spanner several times can yield a bipartite spanner.
Lemma 2.1 (Bipartite spanner, Klein [68]). Let G be a graph, and let Q be a shortest path in G
from a to b, and let Q′ be an arbitrary path from a to b. Then for any ε > 0 there is a subgraph H
with |H| = O(1/ε3)|Q′| such that for any q ∈V (Q) and q′ ∈V (Q′) we have distH∪Q∪Q′(q, q′) ⩽
(1 + ε)distG(q, q′).
Perimeters in region-restricted graphs
Let G be an intersection graph, and let eR be a wire-
frame of G that is a cycle, where R is either the bounded or unbounded region defined by γ(eR). Con-
sider now the graph R := G|R: for each vertex ˙v ∈V (eR) there is a natural corresponding polygon
vR ∈V (G|R), namely, the component of p(˙v) ∩R that contains the point ˙v. Consider the polygon set
{vR | ˙v ∈V (eR)}. Notice that because of the definition of wireframes, whenever ˙v ˙v′ is an edge of eR,
then vRv′
R is an edge induced by {vR | ˙v ∈V (eR)}. Let Perim(R) denote the walk on {vR | ˙v ∈V (eR)}
corresponding to eR.
42


## Page 46


Object duplication
Let us discuss a technical issue that can be avoided using a simple trick.
Starting here, we will often use a path eP in some wireframe f
W of G to define a path in G. This is not
straightforward, since eP would normally give rise to a walk in G through the parent relation: indeed,
if ˙u˙v is an edge of eP, then p( ˙u)p(˙v) is an edge in G, but different vertices along eP may have the same
parent in G. To avoid technicalities involving walks using an object multiple times, we pretend that we
have multiple copies of an object available. In all cases where v ∈G appears k times as a parent along
the walk corresponding to eP, we add k −1 new copies of the polygon v to G. This is also done in a
restricted intersection graph R := G|R whose boundary is a wireframe cycle eR: we think of the objects
of Perim(R) as objects of R, each of which has been copied as many times as they occur as parents
of V (eR)-vertices. Consequently Perim(R) forms a cycle in G|R rather than a closed walk. Finally, we
remark that adding such objects to G does not impact the optimum of Steiner Tree (or even Subset
TSP). To see why, notice that (sub)polygon(s) of an original object v that appear in the optimum of the
modified instance can be substituted by a single copy of v in any feasible solution, whose total size
can therefore not increase, while any feasible solution of the original instance is also feasible in the
modified instance.
Building a skeleton
Every subpath of a shortest path is a shortest path between its endpoints,
but if a path is, say, twice as long as the distance between its endpoints, then a subpath can be still
much longer than the distance between the endpoints of the subpath. We will often encounter paths
that are approximately short in a sense that extends to subpaths as well:
Definition 8.3. A path P in a graph G from u to v is called an internally c-approximate shortest path
if for any a, b ∈V (P) where a, b are not both endpoints of P 6, we have |P[a, b]| ⩽c · distG(a, b).
The following lemma relates the graphs obtained by restricting G to smaller regions. Essentially,
it says in various forms that distances can only increase with further restrictions. It will be used much
later. Recall that for a cycle W and u, v ∈V (W) we denote by W[u, v] the shorter subpath of W
connecting u and v.
Lemma 8.4. Let eB, eR be wireframe cycles in G where B ⊂R, and let B = G|B and R = G|R. Then the
following hold.
(i) Let u, v ∈V (B) such that u ∈CComp(ˆu ∩B) and v ∈CComp(ˆv ∩B) for some ˆu, ˆv ∈V (R).
Then distB(u, v) ⩾distR(ˆu, ˆv).
(ii) If eP ⊂eB is path such that the corresponding path PR in R is a internally c-approximate shortest
path for some c ⩾1, then the path of Perim(B) corresponding to eP is a internally c-approximate
shortest path in B.
(iii) If f
W is a boundaried wireframe of B then for any ˙u, ˙v ∈V (f
W) we have
distf
W ( ˙u, ˙v) ⩾distB(p|B( ˙u), p|B(˙v)).
Moreover, each of the above statements hold when replacing R with G.

Our final ingredient in the proof of Theorem 8.1 is the creation of a wireframe g
Skel of G called the
skeleton. The skeleton will be created by iteratively adding so-called shortcuts to a starting graph.
Definition 8.5 (Shortcut and its reach). A shortcut of G|R is a shortest path P in G|R between some
vertices ˘u, ˘v ∈V (Perim(R)), such that |P| < |eR[ ˙u, ˙v]|
1+ε . The reach of a shortcut from ˘u to ˘v is the length
of the shorter subpath of eR from ˙u to ˙v, that is, |eR[ ˙u, ˙v]| = |Perim(R)[˘u, ˘v]|.
We need a skeleton with the following properties.

(1 + O(ε))|Q[u, v]|. Since the internal vertices of Q[u, v] are inside the skeleton face, they cannot
be terminals, thus Q[u, v] is a shortest path, and in particular, |Q[u, v]| = distG(u, v). Let Q′ =
(u, u′) ∪P ∪(v′, v), where P is the shortest u′ →v′ path in G[S S]|F. We claim that |Q′| ⩽(1 +
ε)distG(u, v) + 4. We distinguish three cases.
Case 1. Both u′ and v′ are in North(F).
Then by Sk5 we know that North(F) is a shortest path in F. Thus the triangle inequality implies
|Q′| ⩽|North(F)[u′, v′]| + 2 = distF (u′, v′) + 2 ⩽|Q| + 4 = distG(u, v) + 4,
where the second inequality uses that the internal vertices of Q are inside F.
Case 2. Both u′ and v′ are outside North(F), i.e., they are internal vertices of South(F).
Consider the path ¯Q := (u′, u) ∪Q[u, v] ∪(v, v′). By Sk5 we have that South(F) is a internally
(1 + ε)-approximate shortest path in F, thus (1 + ε)| ¯Q| > |South(F)[u′, v′]|. Therefore
|Q′| ⩽|South(F)[u′, v′]| < (1 + ε)| ¯Q| = (1 + ε)(distG(u, v) + 2) < (1 + ε)distG(u, v) + 4.
Case 3. Exactly one of u′ and v′ lies in North(F).
By the spanning property of the bipartite spanner, we have that
|Q′| ⩽(1 + ε)distG(u′, v′) ⩽(1 + ε)(distG(u, v) + 2) ⩽(1 + ε)distG(u, v) + 4.
Recall that distG(u, v) ⩾2/ε −2 since x is on the shortest path from u to v and has distance at
least 1/ε from both u′ and v′. Since ε < 0.5 implies distG(u, v) > 1/ε, we get
|Q′| ⩽(1 + ε)distG(u, v) + 4 < (1 + 5ε)distG(u, v).
This concludes the proof.
8.1
Algorithm for Subset TSP
It can be shown that contracting an edge can decrease the optimum value of Subset TSP only by at
most 2: a closed walk in the modified instance can be extended to a closed walk of the original graph
by traversing the contracted edge at most twice. Thus consolidating a set X of vertices decreases the
optimum by at most 2|X|. To obtain more efficient algorithms, we show that contracting a bounded
number of cliques has only a bounded effect on the value of the optimum solution.
Lemma 8.7. Let T ⊂V (G) be a set of terminals and X ⊂V (G) an arbitrary set, and fix a clique
partition P of G. Then SubsetTSP(G, T) ⩽SubsetTSP(G X, TX) + |T ∩X| + O(|X|), where
TX = V (G X)|T is the set of vertices in G X whose preimage in G contains a terminal from T, and X
is the set of cliques in P that contain some vertex of X. Moreover, given an optimum tour for (G X, TX), a
tour of length SubsetTSP(G, T)+O(|X|) that is feasible for (G, T) can be constructed in poly(|V (G)|)
time.

on W. We will show how to build a subset TSP walk WG for (G, T) that is potentially longer than
required, and shorten it later. First, we add all arcs of W to WG where at least one endpoint is outside
X. Consider a connected component Y induced by X and let z ∈V (G X) be the vertex that results
from contracting this component; suppose moreover that z appears on W. Then for each consecutive
arc pair uz and zv of W we add a shortest u →v path to WG whose internal vertices are in Y .

whose shared vertex is z, the walk WG correctly traverses Y . We repeat the above procedure for all
connected components of G[X] whose contraction appears on W. Notice that the resulting tour WG
is a feasible subset TSP tour of (G, T).

We are now ready to present our approximation scheme for Subset TSP.
Theorem 1.4 (Geometric Subset TSP EPTAS via spanner). Given ε > 0, an intersection graph G
of δ-similarly sized β-fat polygons with its representation, and a set T ⊆V (G) of terminals,

SubsetTSP(G, T) and SubsetTSP(G′, T) ≤(1 + ε)SubsetTSP(G, T) in polynomial time.

2Oβ,δ(1/ε5 log(1/ε))poly(n).

9
The mortar graph
This section aims to construct a so-called mortar graph for a given intersection graph G and terminal
set T. Mortar graphs were introduced by Borradaile et al. [24] for the planar Steiner tree problem. We
also only need them for our Steiner tree algorithms. Our construction is much more involved as we
need to avoid unwanted crossings between columns.
Definition 9.1 (Mortar graph and bricks). A 2-connected wireframe f
M of G is a mortar graph if
f
M has a unique irrelevant face with region Firr ⊂R2, such that any object of V (G) intersecting Firr
also intersects R2 \ Firr. We call all other faces of f
M (as closed subsets of R2) brick regions, and each
brick region B defines a corresponding brick B := G|B.
Definition 9.2 (Compass). Given a mortar graph f
M, a compass of f
M is a partition of the boundary
cycle eB for each brick region B into six paths:
South(eB), East−(eB), East|(eB), North(eB), West|(eB), West−(eB),
so that these subpaths of eB are in anticlockwise order if B is a bounded brick region and in clockwise
order if B is the unique unbounded brick region. We further define
West(eB) := West−(eB) ∪West|(eB)
and
East(eB) := East−(eB) ∪East|(eB).

9.1
Defining bridges
A shortest path of R is a bridge if it is a shortest path of length at most 7 connecting γ(South(eR))
to γ(North(eR)), that is, a shortest path in R with vertices v1, . . . , vk where k ⩽8, v1 intersects
γ(South(eR)), and vk intersects γ(North(eR)). We note that the parents of the endpoints of North(eR)
and South(eR) are considered to be length-0 bridges. Consider a bridge P. Since it intersects the
northern and southern boundary of R, its first vertex must also intersect some u ∈V (North(R))
and its last vertex must intersect some u′ ∈V (South(R)) (where v1 = u and vk = u′ are possible),

creating a path of length at most 9 connecting u and u′. Let ˙u, ˙u′ be the vertices of eR such that p( ˙u) = u
and p( ˙u′) = u′. We invoke Corollary 4.3 to make a shortest path connecting u and u′ as terminals such
that the corresponding objects of the object frame contain ˙u and ˙u′. The corresponding wireframe
is a bridge path connecting ˙u and ˙u′, and it splits the region R into two parts: a western and eastern
subregion. (Note that the wireframe eR could now be extended with the edges and new vertices of the
bridge path.)
Next, we define a greedy collection of bridges as follows. Consider the vertices of South(R) in a
west-to-east order, denoting its first and last vertex by v and w, and let P1 be the trivial bridge made
up by v. Suppose that Pi is a bridge already defined, then let Pi+1 be a bridge whose starting point is
the first on South(R)[v, w] whose objects are disjoint from the objects of Pi. This defines some greedy
collection of pairwise disjoint bridges indexed according to their west-to-east order with the property
that if u ∈South(R) is between the starting points of Pi and Pi+1, then any bridge starting at u has
an object intersecting either some object of Pi or some object of Pi+1. For each of these bridges let us
fix corresponding bridge paths ePi, which subdivides R into a sequence of smaller regions. In such a
smaller region R′ we define South(eR′) and North(eR′) to be the parts of eR′ that fall on South(eR) and
North(eR), respectively.
A vertex v of R′ = G|R′ is called boundary-distant if distR′(v, V (South(R′) ∪North(R′)) > λ.
(Recall that λ = 1/ε7.5). Consider now two consecutive small regions R′ and R′′ divided by some bridge
path ePi. If neither of R′ and R′′ have a boundary-distant vertex, then we remove the bridge path ePi. We
claim that the resulting region R′∪R′′ still does not contain a boundary-distant vertex. First, if a vertex
v ∈V (R′) disjoint from γ( ePi) has distance kv to V (South(R′) ∪North(R′) in R′, then its distance to
the same set of vertices is not larger in R′′′ := G|R′∪R′′, and notice that V (South(R′) ∪North(R′) ⊂
V (South(R′′′) ∪North(R′′′). The analogous property holds for any v ∈V (R′′) disjoint from γ( ePi).
Thus if R′′′ has a boundary-distant vertex v, then it must intersect γ( ePi). This however implies that
v ∈NR′′′p( ePi) and thus v is within distance 10 from the starting vertex of Pi in R′′′. Since λ > 10,
we conclude that R′′′ cannot have a boundary-distant vertex.
We continue eliminating bridges between consecutive regions lacking a boundary-distant vertex.

10
The structure theorem and spanner for Steiner tree
Let G ∈Iα be a graph, and suppose T ⊂V (G) and ε > 0 are given. Suppose that we have constructed
a mortar graph M in G using Theorem 9.3. We will set Gorig := G, and then extend G with all
restricted objects in all bricks B of M. As a result, the optimum solution in G is unchanged (see also
the discussion on Page 43 around object duplication), but the size of G might be much bigger than the
original graph Gorig, and we occasionally need to refer to only original fat objects, as G now typically
contains non-fat subpolygons of objects.
10.1
Simplifying a forest in a large brick
Recall that every large brick is a part of some lens. We call the vertices of NB(V (∂B)), λ) outer vertices,
and vertices of V (B) \ NB(V (∂B), λ) will be called inner vertices of B. Recall that small bricks do
not have boundary-distant vertices and thus they do not have inner vertices.
Suppose that A is a vertex set in a thick brick B. Based on A, we will define a wireframe eQA, whose
outer face is the concatenation of the paths South( eQA), East( eQA), North( eQA), West( eQA). The graph
62


## Page 66


eQA will consist of the paths South(eB) (equal to South( eQA)), North(eB) (equal to North( eQA)), and a
forest eFA. We define the wireframe eQA such that it satisfies the following conditions.
(P1) The path North( eQA) is a shortest path in eQA.
(P2) For some k ⩽κ there is a set of vertices s0, . . . , sk ordered from west to east on South( eQA) such
that for any vertex x of South( eQA)[si, si+1) we have
|South( eQA)[si, x]| ⩽εdist e
QA(x, North( eQA)).
(P3) For any pair of vertices a, b of South( eQA) where a, b are not both endpoints of South( eQA),
the path along South( eQA) is an approximate shortest path, that is, |South( eQA)[a, b]| ⩽(1 +
ε)dist e
QA(a, b).
We can therefore apply the following theorem in a black-box fashion. Given a subgraph eF of eQA a
joining vertex of eF is a vertex that has an incident edge both in eF and eQA \ eF.
Theorem 10.1 (Borradaile et al., Thm. 10.7 [24]). Let eQA be a plane graph satisfying conditions9
(P1)-(P3). Let eFA = E( eQA) \ E(South( eQA) ∪North( eQA)). Then there is a forest eF of eQA with the
following properties.
(F1) If two vertices of North( eQA) ∪South( eQA) are connected in eFA then they are connected in eF.
(F2) The number of joining vertices of eF on North( eQA) ∪South( eQA) is o(1/ε5.5).
(F3) The total length of eF is at most (1 + O(ε))| eFA|.
Remark 10.2. The forest eF in Theorem 10.1 joins the northern and southern boundary of eB at only
o(1/ε5.5) vertices. Assume without loss of generality that we remove any component from eF whose
intersection with North( eQA) ∪South( eQA) is disjoint from the eFA ∩North( eQA) ∪South( eQA). This
preserves the properties required of eF. Consider a maximal subpath eI of North( eQA)∪South( eQA) that
is also a subpath of eF. Either there is a joining vertex of eF on eI, or the component of eF containing
I must be I itself. Moreover, if two such disjoint maximal intervals I1, I2 of eF ∩E(South( eQA) ∪
North( eQA)) are in different components of eF, then no vertex pair u ∈I1, v ∈I2 can be connected
in eFA: indeed, such a connection would also have to be present in eF. Thus the number of maximal
intervals of eF in eQA is at most o(1/ε5.5) plus the number of connected components of eFA.
In what follows, we are generally concentrating on a single brick B. Let ∂0 = V (South(B) ∪
North(B)) and we use the shorthand ∂1 for the set of objects in V (B) that intersect the curve
γ(North(eB)) or the curve γ(South(eB)). Let ∂2 = NB(∂1 ∪∂0). Consequently, ∂0 ⊆∂1 ⊆∂2.

In order to simplify a forest in a brick via Theorem 10.1, we will need the following.
Lemma 10.3. Let A be a vertex set in B\∂1. Then the wireframe eQA defined above satisfies the conditions
(P1)-(P3).
Proof. Condition (P1) can be easily proven using the construction of eQA. Recall that eQA is a (bound-
aried) wireframe of B, so by Lemma 8.4(iii) the distance between two vertices of eQA are at least as
big as the distance of their parents in B. On the other hand, North( eQA) = North(eB) is a path that
corresponds to a shortest path in B by M2, thus North(eB) is a shortest path in any wireframe inside
B that contains it.
We can analogously argue that Condition (P2) follows from M6 and Condition (P3) follows
from M2.
We will also need the following simple lemma.
Lemma 10.4. The forest eFA = E( eQA) \ E(South( eQA) ∪North( eQA)) has (1 + O(ε))|A| vertices.
Proof. By Theorem 4.2 the forest | eFA| has |A|+O(t) vertices, where t is the number of point terminals

10.3
Stating and proving the structure theorem
We will now state and prove our structure theorem for Steiner trees. Roughly, our theorem states that
there exists a (1 + ε)-approximate solution where in each brick either no inner points are used, or
when they are used, then they are from an optimum Steiner forest of some portal family of the brick.
We need some definitions first.
Definition 10.5 (Portals). Given G, ε > 0 and a mortar graph f
M, we designate 2/ε7.5 = 2λ vertices
of Perim(B) as portals in each brick B of f
M. The portals p1, p2, . . . on Perim(B) are placed at (almost)
equal distances along the cycle Perim(B), that is, the distances between consecutive portals along
Perim(B) differ by at most 1.
It follows that in any brick B and any vertex v of Perim(B) there is a portal pi such that
distPerim(B)(v, pi) ⩽1
λ|Perim(B)|. We will usually denote by PB the set of portals assigned in B.
Definition 10.6 (Portal-respecting generator). Given Gorig ∈Iα, a mortar graph f
M and portals
PB in each brick B of f
M, a portal-respecting generator is a pair (S0, F) where S0 ⊂NG(p(V (f
M)), λ),
and F is a sequence of portal families FB, one for each brick B of f
M, such that each FB consists of
pairwise disjoint subsets of PB of size at least 2. The pair (S0, F) generates all sets S that arise as the
union of S0 and for each brick B and each portal set U ∈FB an arbitrary optimum Steiner tree of
(B, U). The cost of the generator (S0, F) is

Definition 10.7 (Feasible portal-respecting generator). Given Gorig ∈Iα, a mortar graph f

a terminal set T ⊂V (G)orig, the feasibility graph of the portal-respecting generator (S0, F) is a graph
G(S0, F) that consists of G[S0 ∪S

connected and its vertex set (more precisely,10 V (G(S0, F))orig) contains T.
We note that in the above definition the size of every generated set is at most the cost of the
generator. We are now ready to state our main structure theorem for Steiner Trees.
Theorem 10.8 (Structure theorem for Steiner Trees). Let G be an intersection graph of similarly
sized connected fat objects, and suppose a terminal set T ⊂V (G) and ε > 0 are given. Then in polynomial
time we can construct a mortar graph f
M of G for the terminal set T and corresponding portal set, such
that there exists a feasible portal-respecting generator (S0, F) of cost at most (1 + O(ε))StTree(G, T).

Proving that (S0, F) is feasible.
To prove the feasibility, we must ensure that the feasibility graph G(S0, F) is connected and contains

Since terminals have corresponding vertices in f

boundary ∂B. Thus it would be sufficient to prove that the endpoint pairs of such subpaths remain
connected in in G(S0, F). In fact, in order to show connectivity, within B, we simply consider all
the subpolygons of each object of G(S0, F) that fall in the region B as part of G(S0, F): this does
not influence the connectedness of G(S0, F). Let B(S0, F) denote the resulting graph; we note that
B(S0, F) contains a clique on each U ∈FB . (Note that we will not use these objects when we will
bound the cost of (S0, F).)

Next, we simplify our structure theorem further by slicing the Steiner trees generated by our portal-
respecting generator into shortest paths whose vertices come from a restricted vertex set. First, we
define the variant of a generator based on shortest paths.

Definition 10.10 (Path generator). Given Gorig ∈Iα and a mortar graph f
M a path generator is
a pair (S0, W) where S0 ⊂NG(p(V (f
M)), λ), and W is a sequence of triples (B, u, v) where B is a
brick of f
M and u, v ∈B. The pair (S0, W) generates all sets S that arise as the union of S0 and for
each triplet (B, u, v) an arbitrary shortest path from u to v in B. Let VW denote the set of vertices
appearing in W. The cost of the generator (S0, Q) is
|S0| + |VW| +

(distB(u, v) −1).
We observe that the size of a set S generated from (S0, W) is at most the cost of the generator.
Definition 10.11 (Feasible path generator). Let (S0, W) be a path generator for Gorig ∈Iα, a
mortar graph f
M, and let T ⊂V (Gorig) be a given terminal set. The feasibility graph of the path
generator (S0, W) is a graph G(S0, W) that consists of G[S0 ∪VW] together with the edges uv for
each (B, u, v) ∈Q. The path generator (S0, W) is feasible if G(S0, W) is connected and its vertex set
(more precisely,11 V (G(S0, W))orig) contains T.
With this definition we can state a modified structure theorem for path generators.
Theorem 10.12 (Structure theorem with path generators). Let G be an intersection graph of sim-
ilarly sized connected fat objects, and suppose a terminal set T ⊂V (G) and ε > 0 are given. Then in
2O(1/ε7.5)poly(n) time we can construct a mortar graph f
M of G for the terminal set T and a vertex set
W ⊂V (G) with the following properties:
(i) |W ∩B| = 2O(1/ε7.5) for each brick B of f
M
(ii) For each w ∈W ∩B we have distB(w, Perim(B)) ⩽|Perim(B)|.
(iii) there exists a feasible path generator (S0, W) with
(a) cost at most (1 + O(ε))StTree(G, T)
(b) VW ⊂W
(c) |VW ∩B| = O(1/ε7.5) for each brick B of f
M.
(d) For each brick B there are at most O(1/ε7.5) triplets in W involving B.
Proof. Let f
M be a mortar graph of G for the terminal set T that is computed in polynomial time using
Theorem 9.3. For each brick B, let PB denote its portal set, which we compute in polynomial time
based on f
M. For any subset Q ⊂PB let Can(Q) be a canonical tree which is an optimal Steiner tree
for the instance (B, Q) that we compute using the algorithm of Dreyfus and Wagner [53]. Note here
that Perim(B) is a feasible solution to the instance (B, Q) and thus |Can(Q)| ⩽|Perim(B)|. The
algorithm of Dreyfus and Wagner [53] finds a solution in 3|Q|poly(|V (B)|) = 2O(λ)poly(|V (B)|)
time. Computing all the canonical trees for each of the 2O(λ) portal sets in each brick thus takes
2O(λ)poly(n) time.
Next, for each canonical tree Can(Q) fix a spanning tree SP(Q) of B[Can(Q)]. Let V̸=2(Q) denote
the set of vertices in SP(Q) whose degree is not 2 (i.e., this is the set of leaves and branching vertices
of the spanning tree). Let W be the union of the sets V̸=2(Q) for all sets Q ⊂PB with |Q| ⩾2 over all
bricks B of f
M. Since each tree SP(Q) has at most |Q| ⩽|PB| ⩽2λ leaves, we have that |V̸=2(Q)| ⩽
2λ + 2λ −1 < 4λ. Since there are at most 22λ subsets Q, there are altogether 4λ · 22λ = 2O(λ)
vertices of B that have been added to W, i.e., |W ∩B| = 2O(λ) and W satisfies (i). Notice moreover
that Can(Q) has a subpath connecting each w ∈W ∩B to Perim(B), so distB(w, Perim(B)) ⩽
|Can(Q)| ⩽|Perim(B)|, thus (ii) holds.
Theorem 10.8 implies that there exists a feasible portal-respecting generator (S0, F) of cost at most
(1 + O(ε))StTree(G, T). Let S be a set generated by (S0, F). Recall that |S| is at most the cost of

As a corollary, we can find an approximate solution in the union of the mortar graph, of the set of

Theorem 10.13 (Steiner tree spanner for intersection graphs).
Let G be an intersection graph of
similarly sized connected fat objects with terminal set T and ε > 0 given. Then in time 2O(1/ε7.5)poly(n)
we can compute an induced subgraph G′ of G such that G′ has a Steiner tree spanning T of size at most
(1 + ε)StTree(G, T), and G′ consists of at most 2O(1/ε7.5)StTree(G, T) cell cliques of G.
Proof. Let f
M be a mortar graph of G for the terminal set T that is computed in polynomial time using
Theorem 9.3. Use Theorem 10.12 to construct a vertex set W where |W ∩B| = 2O(1/ε7.5). For all
vertex pairs u, v ∈W ∩B we consider a shortest path from u to v in B. By Theorem 10.12(ii), we have
that there exist vertices u′, v′ ∈Perim(B) such that distB(u, u′) ⩽|Perim(B)| and distB(v, v′) ⩽
|Perim(B)|. Moreover, we have distB(u′, v′) ⩽|Perim(B)|, so by the triangle inequality, we have
distB(u, v) ⩽3|Perim(B)|. Thus every shortest path added inside B has length at most 3|Perim(B)|.
Let G′ be the subgraph of G consisting of NG(V (p(f
M)), λ)∪S

10.4
Algorithm for Steiner Tree via contraction decomposition
We start by proving the following lemma, which is analogous to Lemma 8.7.
Lemma 10.14. Let T ⊂V (G) be a set of terminals and X ⊂V (G) an arbitrary set, and fix a clique
partition P of G. Then StTree(G, T) ⩽StTree(G X, TX)+|T ∩X|+O(|X|), where TX = V (G X)|T
is the set of vertices in G X whose preimage in G contains a terminal from T, and X is the set of cliques
in P that contain some vertex of X. Moreover, given an optimum Steiner tree for (G X, TX), a Steiner
tree of size StTree(G, T) + O(|X|) that is feasible for (G, T) can be constructed in poly(|V (G)|) time.
Proof. Let S be the vertex set of an optimum Steiner tree of (G X, TX). We will show how to build a
Steiner tree walk SG for (G, T) that is potentially longer than required, and shorten it later. First, we
add all vertices of S to SG that also appear in G (i.e., the vertices that were not contracted). Consider a
connected component Y induced by X and let z ∈V (G X) be the vertex that results from contracting
this component; suppose moreover that z appears on S. We select a vertex y ∈Y arbitrarily. Then for
each edge uz of S we add a shortest u →y path to SG whose internal vertices are in Y . Additionally,
for any terminal t ∈T that appears in Y , we extend SG with the vertices of a path that connects t and y
inside G[Y ]. Note that the resulting graph contains all terminals in Y . We repeat the above procedure
for all connected components of G[X] whose contraction appears in S. Notice that the resulting vertex
set SG induces a connected graph containing all terminals, thus any spanning tree of SG is a feasible
solution for (G, T).
We now decrease the size of SG. We can apply the simplifications described in Lemma 7.5 to ensure
that in each clique of X the new graph S′
G contains at most O(1) non-terminal vertices. Consequently,
|S′
G| ⩽SubsetTSP(G X, TX) + |T ∩X| + O(|X|).

is a set where from each clique C of P the set contains O(1) non-terminal vertices. Now we contract

Theorem 1.5 (Geometric Steiner Tree EPTAS via spanner). Given ε > 0, an intersection graph G
of δ-similarly sized β-fat polygons with its representation, and a set T ⊆V (G) of terminals,
1. (Steiner-tree spanner) We can find an induced subgraph G′ ⊆G such that |V (G′)| ≤2Oβ,δ(1/ε7.5) ·
StTree(G, T) and StTree(G′, T) ≤(1 + ε)StTree(G, T) in time 2Oβ,δ(1/ε7.5)poly(m).
2. (EPTAS) We can find a (1 + ϵ)-approximate solution of the instance (G, T) in time
22Oβ,δ(1/ε7.5)poly(n).
Proof. We apply Theorem 3.4 to obtain an α-standard graph G ∈Iα whose representation has polyno-
mial complexity. Let P be the corresponding cell partition. By Lemma 7.8 we may assume without loss
of generality that for each C ∈P we have |C ∩T| ⩽1/ε and |C| ⩽2O((1/ε) log(1/ε)) (after spending
2O((1/ε) log(1/ε))poly(n) time to compute this).
Let opt denote the number of vertices in the optimal Steiner tree of (G, T). We use Theorem 10.13
to compute a subgraph G∗with at most 2γλopt cell cliques for some constant γ in time 2O(λ)poly(n)
that contains a (1 + ε)-approximate solution. This is a Steiner tree spanner of the desired size, and
thus proves the first part of the theorem.
Let A denote the set of at most 2γλopt cell cliques in G∗. We have that the optimum opt∗of G∗
satisfies opt∗⩽(1 + O(ε))opt. It is now sufficient to compute a (1 + O(ε))-approximate tour in G∗
for the terminal set T.
We apply Theorem 6.1 on G∗and the cell partition A for k = 2γλ
ε
= 2O(λ), which yields a col-
lection X1, . . . , Xk ⊂S and corresponding vertex sets X1, . . . , Xk. Note that ℓ= maxC∈S |C| ⩽
2O((1/ε) log(1/ε)).
Let us now compute an optimum Steiner tree for each of the instances (G∗Xi, TXi), where
TXi = V (G∗Xi)|T . By Theorem 6.1 each Xi satisfies tw(G∗Xi) = O(kl) = 2O(λ). We can there-
fore apply the 2O(tw)poly(n) algorithm [20] to solve these instances in 22O(λ)poly(n) time. Note that
StTree(G∗Xi, TXi) ⩽opt∗because contractions can only decrease the optimum cost of a subset
TSP instance.
By Lemma 10.14 in polynomial time we can construct trees τi for (G∗, T) of length at most
StTree(G∗, T) + O(|Xi|) = opt∗+ O(|Xi|).
Theorem 6.1 guarantees that Pk
i=1 |Xi| = O(|A|), and therefore there exists some i where |Xi| =
O(|A|/k) = O(ε) · opt. As a result, the shortest among the trees τi has length at most opt∗+ O(ε) ·
opt = (1 + O(ε)) · opt.
11
A more efficient approximation scheme for Steiner Tree
The main goal of this section is to prove a theorem that transforms our input instance into vertex-
weighted instance that is easier to handle algorithmically.
Formally, the vertex-weighted variant is defined as follows.
Vertex-Weighted Steiner Tree
Input: A graph G, a vertex weighting ω : V (G) →N and a vertex set T ⊂G called
terminals.
Output: The minimum k ∈N such that there exist a vertex set S ⊆V (G) of weight
ω(S) ⩽k where T ⊂S and G[S] is connected.
Most of this section is devoted to proving the following theorem.
Theorem 11.1. Given G ∈Iα, T ⊂V (G) and ε > 0, in 2O(1/ε7.5)poly(n) time we can construct a
graph G′, a terminal set T ′ ⊂V (G′), a vertex weighting ω : V (G′) →N, and a partition P′ of V (G′)
such that the following hold.
72


## Page 76


(i) For each class C′ ∈P′ we have |C′| ⩽2O(1/ε7.5).
(ii) tw(G′ P′) = O(1/ε18), and a tree decomposition of G′ P′ of width O(1/ε18) can be constructed
in polynomial time.
(iii) Given a Steiner tree of (G′, ω, T ′) of weight κ, we can construct a Steiner tree of (G, T) of size
κ + O(ε)StTree(G, T) in polynomial time.
(iv) There exists a Steiner tree S′ of (G′, ω, T ′) with the following properties:
(a) The weight of S′ is at most (1 + O(ε))StTree(G, T).
(b) For each C′ ∈P′ we have |S′ ∩C′| ⩽O(1/ε7.5).
To prove Theorem 11.1, we start by applying Lemma 7.8 to get an instance that is equivalent to
(G, T) where all classes in P have size at most 2O(1/ε log(1/ε)), and each class of P contains at most
O(1/ε) terminals from T.
Our proof proceeds throughout this section. For our input graph Gorig ∈Iα recall that P denotes

have size at most 2O(1/ε log(1/ε)); we will keep using the notation (G, T) for this modified instance. Let
opt = StTree(G, T).

11.2
A contraction decomposition that avoids Yτ
We will need a bound on the treewidth of certain plane graphs. The radial graph Radial(G) of a plane
graph G is the bipartite graph where vertices are either in V (G) or they are obtained by adding a
vertex vF to each face F of G. A vertex vF is then connected to v ∈V (G) in Radial(G) if v appears
on the face F. Let VF denote the set of vertices corresponding to faces. The dual graph Gdual of G is a
plane multigraph on VF where two vertices are connected by k edges if and only if the corresponding
faces have k shared edges in G. We note that for a connected graph G we have that (Gdual)dual is
isomorphic to G and Radial(Gdual) is isomorphic to Radial(G). A result of Demaine et al. [50] gives
the following bound.
75


## Page 79


Lemma 11.5 (Corollary of [50]). For any plane graph G we have that tw(G ∪Radial(G)) =
O(tw(G)).
Proof. Demaine et al. [50] proved that tw(Hdual ∪Radial(H)) = tw(Hdual ∪Radial(Hdual)) =
O(tw(Hdual)) for any plane graph H. For each connected component Gi of G let H = Gdual
i
, and
apply the above result to get tw(Gi ∪Radial(Gi)) = O(tw(Gi)). Now a tree decomposition of G ∪
Radial(G) of width O(maxi tw(Gi)) = O(tw(G)) can be obtained as follows. Remove the vertex of
Radial(Gi) corresponding to the outer face of Gi from each bag, and add the vertex vG corresponding
to the outer face of G to every bag; let Ti be the obtained tree decomposition. Finally, a singleton bag
containing only vG is connected to an arbitrary bag of each tree decomposition Ti. This is a valid tree
decomposition of G ∪Radial(G) of width 1 + O(maxi tw(Gi)) = O(tw(G)).

Let S′ be a Steiner tree for the instance
(G′, ω, T ′ = T X ∪) of weight κ = ω(S′). We first remove vertices from S′ in order to make it a

non-terminal vertex of degree 2 without both of its neighbors would contradict the minimality of S′.
Now, for each vertex v ∈S′ ∩V (G) we add v to the set S. Consider a vertex x ∈S′ ∩ψ(X∪) and
the corresponding vertex set Ax ∈X ∪where ψ(Ax) = x. We add the set Ax to S. Now we process
the remaining vertices, which is V (Jump(y) \ NAW(y)) ∩S′ for each y ∈ψ(Y ∪). For each vertex
v ∈S′ ∩(AW(y)) we simply add the corresponding ν(v) ∈V (G) to S. If v = mQ for some Y -slice
Q from a to b, then we add all vertices of Q(a, b) into S. Notice that S gains at most ω(mQ) internal
vertices that are not in Z∪, and all weight 1 vertices of S′ grew S by at most one vertex.
Finally, we extend S with all vertices of Z∪. Notice that G[S] is not necessarily connected, and it
may be very large, as Z∪is significantly larger than Z. However, we observe that |S \ Z∪| ⩽ω(S′).
