11.5
A faster EPTAS for Steiner Tree
The following lemma presents a general way to compute Steiner trees in a graph with a partitioned
vertex set. Let G be graph and let R be a partition of V (G). We say that a vertex set S is (k, R)-bounded
if for each C ∈R we have |C ∩S| ⩽k.
Lemma 11.13. Let G be an n-vertex graph with nonnegative vertex weighting ω and let R be a partition
of V (G) where each class C of R has at most r vertices. Let K be a given set of terminals, and suppose
that we are given a tree decomposition of G R of treewidth w. Then we can find the minimum weight
(κ, R)-bounded Steiner tree of (G, K) in (rw)O(κw)poly(n) time.
Proof. We modify the standard treewidth-based dynamic programming for Steiner tree to this setting.
Observe that, based on the tree decomposition, we can make a tree decomposition T0 of G by consid-
ering each bag Xt of the given decomposition, and substituting each class C ∈Xt of G R with all
vertices inside C. The resulting tree decomposition is a valid decomposition of G and has width rw.
Observe that the trees we need to consider intersect each class in at most κw vertices.
The standard dynamic programming algorithm for Steiner tree [29, 43] proceeds bottom-up on a
(slightly tweaked) nice tree decomposition. We will rely on the notation of [43, Section 7.3.3.]. For a
bag Xt let Gt denote the subgraph of G induced by the subtree rooted at the bag Xt. Then, for each
X ⊂Xt and partition P of X, there is a subproblem c[Xt, X, P], which is the minimum number of
edges in a forest F in Gt such that:
1. Xt ∩V (F) = X.
2. Every terminal from K ∩V (Gt) is in V (F).
3. The forest F has |P| connected components, and for each class Xi of P there is a corresponding
connected component F i of F such that Xi = V (Fi) ∩Xt.
First, we compute a nice tree decomposition based on T0 in polynomial time using standard tech-
niques [43]; notice that the resulting nice tree decomposition T has bags that are subsets of existing
bags, so in particular, it still holds that for each bag Xt of T there are at most w classes of R intersect-
ing Xt.
We can use the same algorithm with two small modifications. First, we will only iterate over sets
X ⊂Xt where |X ∩C| ⩽κw, as any (k, R)-bounded Steiner tree will intersect Xt in at most κw
vertices. Second, instead of c[Xt, X, P] denoting the number of edges in F, it will denote the minimum
vertex weight of V (F) satisfying the same properties.
85


## Page 89


One can verify that the same dynamic programming algorithm works to compute the minimum
weight (κ, R)-bounded Steiner tree. The original algorithm has |Xt|O(|Xt|) states for the bag Xt, as it
needs to consider all 2|Xt| subsets X and up to |Xt|O(|Xt|) partitions on each subset. The dynamic pro-
gramming needs to consider all pairs of states from two child bags to compute the value for a state, so
the running time is twO(tw)n for an n-vertex graph with a tree decomposition of width tw. In our case,
we only have |Xt|O(kw) = (rw)O(κw) subsets to consider, each of which has (κw)O(κw) ⩽(rw)O(κw)
partitions. Thus the number of states is (rw)O(κw) and the running time is (rw)O(κw)poly(n).
Theorem 1.6 (Faster EPTAS for geometric Steiner Tree). Given ε > 0, an intersection graph G of
δ-similarly sized β-fat polygons with its representation, and a set T ⊆V (G) of terminals, we can find a
(1 + ϵ)-approximate solution in time 2Oβ,δ(1/ε33)poly(n).
Proof. We apply Theorem 3.4 to obtain an α-standard graph G ∈Iα whose representation has poly-
nomial complexity. Apply Theorem 11.1 to obtain a graph (G′, ω, T ′) and vertex partition P′. Theo-
rem 11.1(ii) gives a tree decomposition T ′ of G′ P′ of width w = O(1/ε18). By Theorem 11.1(i) we
have that each class of P′ has size at most r = 2O(1/ε7.5). Finally, by Theorem 11.1(iv)(a),(b) there
exists a solution S′ to the instance (G′, ω, T ′) of weight (1 + O(ε))opt that is (κ, P′)-bounded where
κ = O(1/ε7.5). Thus by Lemma 11.13 we can find a solution S′ of weight at most (1 + O(ε))opt in
(rw)O(κw)poly(n) time, where the first term can be bounded as:
(rw)O(κw) =

2O(1/ε7.5) · O(1/ε17)
O(1/ε7.5)·O(1/ε18)
= 2O(1/ε33).
Using Theorem 11.1(iii) we now construct a solution of (G, T) based on S′ whose weight can be
bounded as
|S| = ω(S′) + O(ε) · opt = (1 + O(ε))opt
where we used that there are at most O(1/ε) terminals in each clique. The dominant term in the
running time is the algorithm of Lemma 11.13, which takes 2O(1/ε33)poly(n) time.
12
Lower bounds and connection to planar problems
12.1
APX-hardness results
The goal of this subsection is to prove the following theorem.
Theorem 1.7 (APX-hardness results). Steiner Tree and Subset TSP are APX-hard in intersection
graphs of:
(a) similarly sized fat (but potentially disconnected) objects, even for unit-diameter 1/3-fat objects with
two connected components,
(b) similarly sized connected (but potentially non-fat) objects, even for unit-diameter objects,
(c) connected fat (but potentially differently sized) objects, even if each object is 0.18-fat, and
(d) similarly sized connected fat objects in R3, even if each object is a unit-diameter ball.
A split graph is a graph whose vertex set can be partitioned into A ˙∪B such that A induces a clique
and B induces an independent set. Our proof of Theorem 1.7 will rely on the fact that two of the above
graph classes are able to represent subdivisions of bounded degree graphs, while the other two are able
to represent arbitrary split graphs.
Lemma 12.1. Steiner Tree and Subset TSP are APX-hard in split graphs.
86


## Page 90


Proof. The traveling salesman problem (TSP) is APX-hard in discrete metric spaces where all distances
are 2 or 3 [80]. One can make an equivalent instance of Subset TSP in a split graph: let M = (X, dist)
be a metric space on n points where distances of distinct points are either 2 or 3. Then let G be a split
graph with a clique C of size
 n
2

+ n indexed by edges of the complete graph on X plus a dummy
vertex dx for each x ∈X. The independent set I of G is of size n and indexed by X. We have an
edge from vertex vx ∈I to wyz ∈C if and only if x ∈y, z and dist(y, z) = 2, and we connect each
vx ∈I to dx ∈C. Since the dummy vertices are neighbors and together with I they form a perfect
matching, we have that any pair of vertices in I has distance at most 3. On the other hand, vx, vy ∈I
has a common neighbor in G if and only if dist(x, y) = 2, and as a result, distG(vx, vy) = 2 holds.
Thus when considering the terminal set T = I in G, the metric of G for the terminals is isomorphic to
M. As a result, a subset TSP tour of (G, T) is in a direct one-to-one correspondence with a TSP tour
of M and they have the same length. Since the reduction is polynomial, this shows that Subset TSP
is APX-hard in split graphs.
For Steiner Tree our reduction is based on the fact that Vertex Cover is APX-hard on connected
graphs of maximum degree 3 [4]. In an instance of vertex cover, we are given some graph G and the
goal is to find a minimum subset of vertices S such that each edge of G is incident to some vertex in
S. Notice that in a connected graph of maximum degree 3 each vertex can cover at most 3 edges, and
there are at least n −1 edges to cover, thus the minimum vertex cover has size at least (n −1)/3.
Based on G, we can build a split graph S = (I, C) where the independent set I has a vertex uxy for
each edge xy of G, and the clique C has a vertex vz for each vertex z of G. We add an edge from vz ∈I
to wxy ∈C if and only if z is incident to the edge xy ∈E(G), that is, z = x or z = y. Let T = I be
the set of terminals, and consider the Steiner Tree instance (S, T). It is easy to see that a vertex cover
of size k in G corresponds to a Steiner tree of size |I| + k in X: namely, we take the terminals T = I
and the vertices in C that correspond to vertices of the vertex cover. On the other hand, any Steiner
tree must contain all terminals (I) and each vertex wxy must be connected to the rest of the tree either
by having vx or vy in the tree, thus the vertices of G corresponding to tree vertices of C form a vertex
cover. This establishes a bijection between vertex covers of G and Steiner trees of G with an additive
size difference of |I|. Notice moreover that n −1 < |I| < 3n/2 and (n −1) ⩽k ⩽n implies that this
is a valid L-reduction. This concludes the proof that Steiner Tree is APX-hard in split graphs.
Our next goal is to prove the following lemma.
Lemma 12.2. Steiner Tree and Subset TSP are APX-hard in graphs of maximum degree 3.
We note that the claim about Subset TSP follows from the APX-hardness of Graphic TSP on the
same graph class [65]; we give an independent proof specifically for Subset TSP.
We will give a PTAS reduction from the same problems in arbitrary graphs. Notice that the op-
timum of both Subset TSP and Steiner Tree in an n-vertex graph is less than 2n. In particular, a
spanning tree of G is a feasible Steiner tree on at most n vertices, and the walk that travels this tree
twice is a feasible subset TSP tour of length 2n −2. Thus for any ε ⩽

An ℓ-subdivision of a graph G is the graph Gt obtained by replacing its edges with paths of length ℓ.
Let S′

We say that a subset TSP walk W of the instance (G, T) is minimal if it can be decomposed into |T|
shortest paths of G that connect T in some cyclic permutation. (Note that one can change any feasible
87


## Page 91


walk W into a minimal feasible walk that is not longer than W in polynomial time.) We claim that
there is a simple bijection between minimal subset TSP walks of (G, T) and (G′, T): one can consider
the length-ℓpaths corresponding to the edges of a minimal walk W of (G, T) of length k: this leads to
a minimal walk W ′ of (G′, T) of length ℓk. Similarly, any minimal walk of (G′, T) consists of shortest
paths between terminal pairs, which in turn are concatenations of length-ℓpaths.
We can now prove the following lemma.
Lemma 12.3. Let G be a graph class where Subset TSP and Steiner Tree are APX-hard. Then for any
positive integer ℓthey are also APX-hard in the graph class Gℓconsisting of the ℓ-subdivisions of the graphs
in G.
Proof. Given the above polynomial-time-computable bijection between minimal feasible Steiner trees
and minimal feasible subset TSP paths of G ∈G and Gℓ∈Gℓ, it remains to show that the approximation
ratio is preserved. In case of subset TSP paths the approximation ratios are identical as the correspond-
ing walk lengths differ by a multiplicative factor of ℓ. For Steiner tree, the function f(k) = ℓ(k−1)+k
is monotone, thus an optimum tree of (G, T) corresponds to an optimum tree in Gℓ, T. If the optimum
tree of (Gℓ, T) has κ −1 paths, then a minimal (1 + ε)-approximate tree of (Gℓ, T) has size at most
(1 + ε)(ℓ(κ −1) + κ). Suppose for the sake of contradiction that this approximate tree has k −1 paths
where k > (1 + ε)κ paths. Then
(1 + ε)(ℓ(κ −1) + κ) ⩾f(k) > f((1 + ε)κ) = (1 + ε)(ℓκ −1) + κ),
which simplifies to −(1+ε)ℓ> −(1+ε), which is a contradiction as ℓ⩾1. Thus the approximate tree
has at most (1 + ε)κ −1 paths, and the corresponding tree has at most (1 + ε)κ vertices, concluding
the proof.
In order to prove Lemma 12.2 we will need to decrease the vertex degrees of graphs using the
following method.
A subcubic dilation of a vertex set S in the graph G replaces each vertex v ∈S with a path Hv on
at least deg(v) vertices, and connects the vertices of NG(v) to distinct vertices of Hv. Consequently,
if G′ is a subcubic dilation of S in G where S contains all vertices of G of degree at least 4, then G′ is
a graph of maximum degree 3 where all vertices of degree 3 are on the paths Hv, and contracting the
paths Hv results in the graph G. We say that a subcubic dilation G′ of S in G is f(n)-bounded if all
paths Hv have at most f(n) vertices.

We can also wrap up the proof of Theorem 1.7 by realizing split graphs and (subdivisions of) graphs
of degree at most 3 as intersection graphs.
89


## Page 93


Proof of Theorem 1.7. We claim that in cases (a) and (d) one can realize some subdivision of any graph
of degree at most 3 that has at least 10 vertices. If we can do this, then Lemma 12.2 and Lemma 12.3
implies that both Steiner Tree and Subset TSP are APX-hard on these graph classes.
Similarly, we claim that in cases (b) and (c) we can realize any split graph, so by Lemma 12.1 we
have that both problems are APX-hard on these intersection graphs.
Let us now define these realizations.
(a) We show that a 28n-subdivision of any graph of maximum degree 3 can be realized as the
intersection graph of the following type of objects. Each object consists of two disks of diameter 1/2,
whose centers are at distance 1 from each other. Moreover, each disk center’s coordinates are integral
or half-integral. These are clearly intersection graphs of similarly sized fat but disconnected objects.
Our strategy is to first construct a drawing of G in a grid (with crossings) where all edges are
represented by paths of equal lengths. We will then replace these paths with the objects in such a
way that crossings in the drawing will be “jumped” over by the corresponding objects. Let G be an
arbitrary graph of maximum degree 3. Let v1, . . . , vn denote its vertices, and let e1, . . . , em denote its
edges where m ⩽3n/2. To construct a grid drawing, we first assign each vertex vi to the grid point
(6i, 0). When vi has degree deg(vi) ⩽3, then vi will be represented by a horizontal segment of length
2(k −1) whose left endpoint is (6i −2, 0), and the grid points (6i −2, 0), (6i, 0), (6i + 2, 0) will be
used as starting points of grid paths representing the edges.
For each j = 1, . . . , m, and corresponding edge ej = vj1vj2 we create a path from one of the
grid points (6j1 −2, 0), (6j1, 0), (6j1 + 2, 0) that are all on the segment of vj1. Similarly, the path is
assigned to one of the grid points (6j2 −2, 0), (6j2, 0), (6j2 + 2, 0) from the segment of vj2. Since the
maximum degree is 3, we can assign distinct grid points from the segment of each vi to distinct edges
incident to vi. Let xj1 < xj2 denote the x-coordinates of these assigned grid points. Then the grid
path Pj corresponding to ej is defined as the union of the following axis-parallel grid path Pj through
the following sequence of grid points:
(6j1, 0), (xj1, 0), (xj1, yj), (xj1 + 1, yj), (xj1 + 1, j), (xj2, j), (xj2, 0), (6j2, 0),
where yj ∈[2n, 14n] is set so that the total length of the path is exactly 14n. See Figure 16. We claim
that such a value y exists. The points (xj1, 0) and (xj2, 0) are both even-coordinate points, and their
horizontal distance is at least 2 and at most 6n, and j ⩽m ⩽3
2n. Thus the path
(6j1, 0), (xj1, 0)(xj1, j), (xj2, j), (xj2, 0), (6j2, 0)
has even length at least 4 (when j = 1 and xj2 −xj1 = 2) and at most 2j + 6n + 4 ⩽9n + 4 < 10n.
The length of Pj is 2(yj −j) longer than this path, therefore the desired yj ∈[2n, 14n] exists such
that the length of Pj is exactly 14n.
Next, we scale the grid drawing by a factor of 2, so that each edge of the original drawing is realized
by two grid edges in the new drawing. Let D denote this new drawing. Consequently, D is a drawing
of the 28n-subdivision of the original graph where crossings can only happen at a point (a, b) when
a, b are both even and b ⩾2.
To realize the subdivision G28n with the disconnected objects (the disk pairs), notice first that the
vertices of the D can be categorized as follows:
C1 vertices of degree 3 (with 2 horizontal edges and one vertical edge on the top)
C2 vertices of degree 2 where both incident edges are horizontal
C3 vertices of degree 2 where both incident edges are vertical
C4 vertices with a vertical incident edge on the top and a horizontal incident edge
C5 vertices with a vertical incident edge on the bottom and a horizontal incident edge

the extra length of 2zj compared to the above paths will ensure that Pj has length exactly 14n.
It is routine to check that the described realization with the paths Pj is an induced grid graph.

Remark 12.5. We crucially leave open the case of intersection graphs of disks (of arbitrary size) and
pseudodisks: neither our algorithm nor our lower bounds extend to these graph classes.
12.2
Reducing planar approximation problems to unit disk graphs
In this section we will prove that there is a PTAS-reduction from Planar Subset TSP to Unit Disk
Subset TSP and from Planar Steiner Tree to Unit Disk Steiner Tree.
Although our reduction is from the unweighted variant, we note that one can easily construct a
PTAS reduction form these problems in the case when the edges have non-negative weights. In this
sense, our algorithms for Subset TSP and Steiner Tree in intersection graphs also implies (E)PTAS
algorithms for the unweighted and weighted variants of these problems in planar graphs.
To prove a formal PTAS reduction, it suffices to prove the following [42]. Given ε > 0, a planar
graph G with terminals T ⊂V (G), we need to construct a unit disk graph G′ and terminal set T ′
in poly(n) time, such that given a (1 + ε)-approximate solution S to (G′, T ′), we can generate a
(1 + cε)-approximate solution to (G, T) in polynomial time, where c is a constant.
Construction of (G′, T ′)
Given ε, G, T, we start by computing a so-called bar visibility layout of G.
In such a layout the vertices of G are represented as horizontal segments whose endpoints have integer
coordinates, and edges are represented as vertical segments whose endpoints have integer coordinates,
and each edge starts and ends on the segments corresponding to its end vertices, and the segments is
disjoint from all other vertex-segments of the drawing.
We will use the following algorithm to compute the desired layout:
Theorem 12.6 (Tamassia and Tollis [78], Rosenstiehl and Tarjan [77]). Given a planar graph G,
a bar visibility layout of G inside the bounding box [1, n] × [1, 2n −4] can be computed in O(n) time.
We apply the transformation f(x, y) = (3n · x, 36n3 · y) on the bar layout to get a representation
in the bounding box. As a result, the vertex-representing segments have length between 3n and 6n2 −
12n, and the edge-representing vertical segments have length between 36n3 and 36n4, while their
horizontal distance is at least 3n, see Figure 17. Using the horizontal space of width of 3n between
consecutive vertical segments, we can change each vertical segment into an induced grid path of length
exactly 36n4 connecting the same pair of endpoints: indeed, a grid rectangle of height 36n3 and width
3n −2 does contain such a path connecting two of its corners. Since the result is an induced grid
graph, it can be represented as a unit disk graph where the disks have diameter 1 and the disk centers
92


## Page 96


are the grid vertices of the drawing. Finally, for each vertex v ∈T we define v′ ∈T ′ to be the unit
disk centered leftmost vertex of the segment corresponding to v in the drawing. For v ∈V (G) let H′
v
denote the set of vertices on the horizontal segment of G′ representing v. Similarly, for each edge uv
of G let S′
uv denote the internal vertices of the grid path representing the edge uv in G′. We can now
observe the following.
Observation 12.7. Given (G, T), the instance (G′, T ′) can be constructed in O(n6) time and the re-
sulting graph has size O(n6). Moreover, G′ is a subcubic dilation of a 36n4-subdivision of G that is
(6n2 −12n)-bounded. In particular, (G′, T ′) satisfies the conditions of Lemma 12.4.
Using our construction and the above observation, we can wrap up the section with the following
theorem.
Theorem 1.8 (Reduction from planar graphs to unit disk graphs). There is a PTAS reduction from
Planar Subset TSP to Unit Disk Subset TSP and from Planar Steiner Tree to Unit Disk Steiner Tree.
Proof. Let k be the number of vertices of the minimum Steiner tree in a planar graph G with terminals
T, and let X′ be the vertex set of a (1 + ε)-approximate solution for the instance (G′, T ′) constructed
above. Then in polynomial time we construct X ⊂V (G). Notice that by Lemma 12.4(i) we have that
the minimum Steiner tree for (G′, T ′) has at most 36(k −1)n4 + ε · 36n4 vertices, thus a (1 + ε)-
approximate solution has size at most |X′| ⩽(1 + ε)(36(k −1)n4 + ε · 36n4) < (1 + ε)k · 36n4. Thus
by Lemma 12.4(ii) we can construct a Steiner tree of size at most (1 + ε)k for (G, T).
Now let k be the length of the minimum subset TSP tour of G for the terminal set T, and let W ′
be a (1 + ε)-approximate closed walk for the instance (G′, T ′) constructed above. Then in polynomial
time we construct the closed walk W using Lemma 12.4(ii). Notice that by Lemma 12.4(i) we have that
the minimum subset TSP for (G′, T ′) has at most 36kn4 + ε · 36n4 edges, thus a (1 + ε)-approximate
solution has size at most |W ′| ⩽(1+ε)(36kn4+ε·36n4) ⩽(1+ε)(k+1)·36n4. Thus Lemma 12.4(ii)
gives that |W| < (1 + ε)(k + 1) −1 < (1 + 2ε)k, concluding the reduction.

A
Converting into α-standard intersection graphs
The following lemmas allow us to turn an intersection graph of similarly sized (e.g. unit) disks or arbi-
trary connected similarly sized fat polygons into an isomorphic intersection graph that is α-standard.
Lemma A.1. Let G be an intersection graph of δ-similarly-sized disks given via its representation. Then in
polynomial time we can build an intersection graph G′ of δ-similarly sized connected (1/
√
2)-fat polygons
with polynomial complexity.
Proof. For each disk d ∈V (G) let S(d) be the maximum axis-parallel square inscribed into d. For
each d, d′ ∈V (G) where d ∩d′ ̸= ∅let pd,d′ be the midpoint of the arc ∂d ∩d′. Let p(d) be the
convex hull of the set σ(d) ∪S
d′∈N(d)\{d} pd,d′. Notice that the vertices of p(d) are on ∂d. Consider
now the intersection graph G′ given by the convex polygons {p(d) | d ∈V (G)}. We claim that
p(d), p(d′) intersect in G′ if and only if d, d′ intersect in G. Since p(d) ⊂d for each d ∈V (G), no new
intersections are created. To check the maintenance of edges, let c(x) denotes the center of x ∈D2.
For each d′ ∈N(d) the intersection between d, d′ is maintained as the segment c(d)pd,d′ intersects the
segment pd′,dc(d′) as both segments are on the line c(d)c(d′), and the ordering of these four points on
the line is c(d), pd′,d, pd,d′, c(d′), where pd′,d and pd,d′ might coincide. It remains to show the fatness
and similar size bound. Notice that σ(d) ⊂p(d) and σ(d) contains an inscribed disk of radius rd/
√
2
where rd is the radius of d, on the other hand p(d) ⊂d, which proves the fatness bound. Finding the
points pd,d′ takes O(n2) time. Using a standard convex hull algorithm [59], the construction of the
polygons p(d) can be executed in O(n2 log n) time, as each p(d) is the convex hull of at most 4+n−1
vertices.
We say that a pair of intersecting segments have a true crossing if they intersect in a single point,
and neither segment contains any endpoint of the other segment.
Lemma A.2. If p and q are simple polygons where any edge e of p and any edge f of q are either disjoint
or have a true crossing, then {p, q} satisfies the second fairness condition, that is, any component of their
intersection is a simple polygon.
Proof. Let x be some connected component of p ∩q. The lemma follows if p ⊂q or q ⊂p as then
x = p or x = q, and p and q are simple polygon; assume that this is not the case, and let A = A(p, q)
be the arrangement given by p and q.
If s is a segment of A in x, then at least one of the two faces of A incident to s must also be in x,
as otherwise s would be a segment of positive length in the intersection of some edge of p and some
edge of q. Moreover, if ˙v ∈∂x is a vertex of ∂x, then either ˙v ∈V (p) ∩Int q, v ∈V (q) ∩Int p, or
˙v is a true crossing. Observe that in each of these cases ˙v has degree 2 in ∂x and has some incident
face of A that is both in p and q. Finally, since p and q are closed, x is closed. We conclude that x is a
non-self-intersecting polygon; it remains to show that it has exactly one boundary component.
Suppose that h is a hole of x, and let f be some edge of h. Then f cannot be on an edge of both
∂p and ∂q, so assume without loss of generality that f ⊂∂p. Since the other side of ∂h (namely, x)
is in p, it follows that the face Ff ⊂Int h of A on the other side of f is not in p. On the other hand,
∂h ⊂x ⊂p, thus Ff is a bounded region outside p that is in the bounded region of the Jordan curve
∂h ⊂p and thus in the bounded region of ∂p. Thus the bounded region of ∂p is not covered by p,
which contradicts the fact that p is a simple polygon.

Lemma A.3. Let G be an intersection graph of δ-similarly-sized β-fat non-self-intersecting polygons,
possibly with holes, given via its representation of polynomial complexity. Then in polynomial time we
can build an intersection graph G′ of δ-similarly-sized (β/4)-fat simple polygons. Moreover, each poly-
gon of G′ is a subpolygon of the corresponding polygon of G, and if G is α-standard, then scaling the
representation of G′ by 4 results in an 4α-standard graph.

is merged into the unbounded face of p′ or some other hole of p′ via Rp,h.

Moreover, diam(p) = diam(p′) (as the diameter of a polygon is realized between some pair of vertices,
and no vertices of p have been removed), and dp ⊂p′. Next, we replace the polygon p in P with p′,
and also update X to include the new points xp,h, x′
p,h.

the connectedness of p: indeed, the top and bottom edge of the trapezoid Rp,h remains connected in p

4 diam(p)
in each polygon p, thus the final polygon set P is a collection of δ-similarly-sized (β/4)-fat simple
polygons. Moreover, the intersection graph is maintained in each step as the polygon p′ replacing p is
a subset of p (thus no new intersections can be created) but all intersections with any other q ∈P are
maintained by p′. Thus the resulting intersection graph G′ is isomorphic to G.
If G was an α-standard graph, then observe that no edges or vertices have been introduced to G′
that could create intersecting edges that do not make a true crossing, and no new concurrent edge
triples are created as new edges avoid existing edges of the arrangement. Lemma A.2 implies that
fairness is maintained. The newly introduced polygons are subpolygons of earlier polygons, and the
maximum inscribed disks decreased by a factor of at most 4. Thus, after scaling the new representation
by 4, the resulting representation is 4α-standard.
It remains to bound the running time of this construction. Notice that covering a polygon p with
a disk requires radius at least diam(p)/2, thus by the fatness bound each polygon contains a disk

polynomial time.
Lemma A.4. Let G be an intersection graph of δ-similarly-sized β-fat simple polygons given via its
representation of polynomial complexity. Then in polynomial time we can build the representation of an
α-standard intersection graph G′ that is isomorphic to G where α = 9/(δβ).
Proof. Let P0 = V (G) be the initial collection of simple polygons. We find a 1/4-approximation din(p)
of the maximum enclosed disk of all polygons p ∈P0 in polynomial time using the same method as
seen in the proof of Lemma A.3. We modify P0 in several steps. First, we scale P0 to ensure each
polygon contains a disk of radius 1 (thus diameter 2), i.e., let P1 be the set of polygons after P0 is
scaled by
2
minp∈P0 diam(din(p)). As a result, every polygon of P1 has diameter at most 8/(βδ). Consider
the arrangement A(P1), and let E1 and V1 denote its set of edges and vertices. We denote by N the total
number of edges in the polygons of P1, and let µ0 the minimum distance among V1 and the minimum
distance between non-incident vertices of V1 and E1. Let µ1 := min{µ0/(100N2), 1/(100N2)}.
We will now modify the polygons so that any pair of edges from distinct polygons intersect in a
point, and no three polygons edges are concurrent. We say that an edge f of the polygon p is bad if
either (a) there is some edge f′ of some polygon p′ where p ̸= p′ and f ∩f′ contains some segment of
positive length (i.e., they overlap) (b) there is some vertex v ∈V (p′) on f from some polygon p′ ̸= p,
or (c) there is some vertex v ∈V1 of degree at least 5 on f.
If a, b are the endpoints of f, then consider the outer angle bisector rays of p at a and b. For each
i = 1, . . . , N let ai and bi be points on the angle bisector from a and b at distance iµ1 from a and b,
respectively, where i ⩽12N2.
Let p(i) be the polygon where a and b are replaced with ai and bi.
Notice that pf(i) stays within distance µ0/4 from p, thus if p, q ∈P1 are disjoint, then pf(i), qf′(j)
are also disjoint for any i, j ∈[12N2]. Moreover, p ⊂pf(i) thus if p intersects q then pf(i) intersects
qf′(j) for all i ̸= j. Finally, note that p and pf(i) differ on three edges, and the curves ∂pf(i) \ ∂p are
pairwise disjoint for different i. Notice also that the polygon p remains simple after the modification.

We now exchange bad edges of P1 one by one so that whenever we replace some polygon p by p(i),
we ensure that all edges on the curve ∂pf(i) \ ∂p are non-bad. We claim that choosing such an index
i ∈[N2] is always possible. First, we observe that the total number N of edges in the polygon collection
is unchanged after such a modification. Consequently, the number of vertices in the arrangement of
the N total polygon edges is always at most

, thus any arrangement of this many edges produces
a planar graph with at most 3

−6 < 2N2 edges. We conclude that there is always a choice of
i ∈[12N2] such that the 3 new edges given by the curve ∂pf(i) \ ∂p are disjoint from all vertices of
the current arrangement and they do not overlap with any edges of the current arrangement. Observe
that a modification eliminates at least one bad edge, and the edges neighboring the modified edge
cannot be bad either. In particular, if a vertex of p is moved, then the incident edges remain good, so
no vertex of p is moved more than once. After at most N steps the resulting polygon collection P2 is
free of bad edges.
By definition, each polygon of P2 includes a disk of radius 1, and they have diameter less than
8/(βδ) + 1/2. Moreover, the intersection graph is maintained throughout the modifications, so the
resulting graph G′ is isomorphic to G.
By Lemma A.2 that G′ satisfies all properties of being α-standard with α = 8/(βδ) + 1/2 <
9/(βδ).
Theorem 3.4. Let G be an intersection graph of (a) δ-similarly sized disks or (b) δ-similarly sized β-
fat polygons given by their representation. Then in polynomial time, we can construct an α-standard
intersection graph G′ that is isomorphic to G whose representation complexity is polynomial in n, where
α = 36/(βδ) and β = 1 for disks.
Proof. In case of (a), use Lemma A.1 to obtain an isomorphic intersection graph of simple polygons
of fatness 1/
√
2 that are δ-similarly sized with representation complexity O(n2). In case of (b), use
Lemma A.3 to obtain an intersection graph of β/4-fat δ-similarly-sized objects. On both of these
graphs, we use Lemma A.4 to get an α-standard graph of with α = 9/(δ
√
2) and α = 36/(βδ),
respectively. Notice that each step increases the complexity of the representation polynomially, thus
the final graph G′ also has poly(n) complexity.
